#6832. Kate Middleton and Prince William Begin Their First Overseas Tour in Four Years

The couple returns to India for the Earthshot Prize, which awards £1 million each to five environmental initiatives.

Kate Middleton and Prince William have arrived in India for a three-day official visit, their first joint overseas tour since 2022. Announced by Kensington Palace over the weekend, the November 16–18 trip is in honor of the sixth annual Earthshot Prize awards in Mumbai. It also marks a significant step in the Princess of Wales’s return to public duties following her 2024 cancer diagnosis.

The couple will attend the awards ceremony on Tuesday, November 17, when five environmental initiatives will each receive £1 million to expand their work. Founded by Prince William in 2020, the prize recognizes solutions across five areas, including restoring nature, reducing waste, and addressing climate change. Mumbai is the awards’ first Indian host city, following past editions in London, Boston, Singapore, Cape Town, and Rio de Janeiro.

The royal itinerary extends beyond the ceremony. Kensington Palace said the visit would highlight sustainable urban transportation, responsible manufacturing, youth-led environmental projects, and India’s creative industries. Today, the couple joined cricket great Sachin Tendulkar and local children for a game of street cricket in Ballard Estate, a historic neighborhood in South Mumbai. The Prince and Princess of Wales are also scheduled to attend the inaugural Children’s Earthshot Awards, recognizing environmental solutions developed by young people ages 9 to 17.

The trip is Catherine’s first overseas tour with William since she disclosed her cancer diagnosis in 2024, and her first appearance at an Earthshot ceremony since the Boston awards in 2022. It is also the couple’s second official visit to India, a decade after their April 2016 tour.

#6827. The 12 Best Round Brushes for a Salon-Level Blowout

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Perfect your next hair look with these expert-loved picks

Perfecting an at-home blowout once required contortionist-level abilities combined with a little patience and a lot of skill, especially when styling the back of your head. But nowadays, thanks to the advancement in hair tools—from high-powered blow-dryers to one of the best round brushes—it's easier than ever to craft sleek styles that rival your local salon without dealing with arm fatigue.

Unlike regular boar-bristle or vented brushes, round brushes feature a cylindrical shape in which hair wraps around for a seamless dry. Though many of these tools are crafted from similar materials, they're also available in a range of sizes to suit your hair texture and unique beauty needs. But regardless of the brush you choose, experts say a polished dry isn't achieved with dripping wet hair. "The key is to get all the moisture out," celebrity hairstylist Priscilla Valles tells Bazaar. "To achieve the smooth look, you need to keep drying until the hair is completely dry."

New York City-based hairstylist Jackson Simmonds recommends having two to three round brushes in your collection—just in case you change your hairstyle. “To keep it simple, think, smaller brushes for shorter lengths, bigger brushes for longer lengths,” he explains.

Here, we've narrowed down some of the best round brushes from Oribe, Sisley Paris, and more, all highly recommended by experts. Whether your hair is short, extra long, curly, or super fine, these top picks can be game-changing to help deliver a bouncy, full-bodied blowout from the comfort of your bathroom.

When we polled hairstylists, several mentioned Olivia Garden's hairbrushes. They win best overall round brush because they're foolproof: They come in a full range of sizes (general rule of thumb: the longer your hair, the larger and longer your barrel should be), and it's hard to get your hair tangled in an Olivia Garden round brush. If you need a place to start, the Ceramic + Ion Thermal Hairbrush is a bestseller. But you can also go through the brand's hairbrush guide to find your perfect fit.

Bristle type: syntheticBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 0.5 inches to 4.25 inches

A boar-bristle round brush is ideal for someone with coarse hair texture, as it provides more stretch and tension than a ceramic or ionic brush. This line is a favorite of stylists like Simmonds, who says, “They have a huge selection of sizes, bristle density, and length, so you have an option for whatever style you're trying to create.” He adds that the cork handle is a plus: “It makes it light and breezy in your hand and never slips away as you're working.”

Bristle type: nylonBarrel material: woodSize options: 35 to 85 millimeters

Oribe crafted a brush that's as efficient as it is pretty. Atop the stunning resin handle is a mix of boar bristles and nylon pins that move through hair without damaging or tugging and help distribute the natural oils in hair for more shine. And although it is an investment (the handle is hand-finished), this is a tool you’ll use for years to come.

Bristle type: boar, nylonBarrel material: Italian resinSize options: medium, large

If you're in search of a round brush with frizz-control benefits, look no further than this top-rated, ergonomic favorite from Drybar. After all, who better to go to than the blowout blueprint? Its bristles are optimized with ionic technology to mitigate frizz while providing salon-level tension control. It even has a thumbprint grip to help maximize your styling experience.

Bristle type: boarBarrel material: ceramicSize options: medium, large

If you're already a fan of Moroccanoil's coveted hair staples, allow us to introduce you to its top-rated ceramic and ion-infused round brush. It heats up when in use with a blow-dryer, making drying and detangling hair a breeze. Designed for medium to long hair, the large barrel covers a good amount of surface area without tugging. Plus, the ceramic finish leaves hair extra-shiny.

Bristle type: nylon, ionicBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 55 millimeters, 45 millimeters

This $14 round brush has earned a five-star rating from beauty lovers. The bristles are heat-resistant vegan boar bristles, and they're pretty densely packed, so you can get a grip and lift on the hair while still allowing strands to glide through without pulling. The bristles also aren’t overly stiff, so they won’t scratch your scalp as you’re styling. And we’re always here for a no-slip grip!

Bristle type: vegan boarBarrel material: bambooSize options: 2.78 inches

These U.K.-made brushes are celebrity stylist Benjamin Mohapi’s personal favorites. “They are the oldest brush company in the world, and I've used them for the entire 40 years of my career,” he explains, adding that the brand has options in boar bristle, vegan bristle, and nylon ceramic. “As far as I’m concerned, there is nothing better.”

Bristle type: nylonBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 45mm and 60mm

Here's a brush from the experts at Sisley Paris that's literally made for your next blowout. Its boar and nylon bristles are gentle yet effective, and compactly housed in a wooden barrel. The soft bristles provide hold for easy drying and also keep the hair super soft. We also appreciate that it has scalp-stimulating bristles, so it feels especially nice when you use it at the root.

Bristle type: boar, nylonBarrel material: woodSize options: medium

This popular round brush proves that getting a polished, salon-level finish doesn't have to cost a ton. Ideal for reducing frizz, the staple uses a ceramic-coated barrel for even heat distribution. Plus, it features an ergonomic handle for extra comfort, which you’ll love whether you’re new to blowing out your hair at home or just need the extra grip. Once you get the hang of things, you may decide to upgrade, but it’s a great starter brush.

The three sturdy knobs on the handle of this round brush mean you don't have to worry about the tool accidentally slipping out of your hands mid-blow-dry. Plus, the placement of the bristles in Philip B.'s tool makes it easy to give your roots an extra lift. With a mix of boar bristles and nylon for extra reinforcement, it’s a sturdy brush that’s earned stellar reviews for good reason.

Can a round brush be chic? This one could be mistaken for a found object with its teak handle. Simmonds recommends the Japanese-made brand, saying that it offers “quality brushes that deliver results, look great coming out of my kit, and last for ages with proper care.” The mix of boar bristle and heat-resistant nylon pins is also ideal for optimal hold and shine.

Bristle type: boar, heat-resistant nylonBarrel material: teak woodSize options: several

Balmain Hair may sound like a fun splurge (and it is), but Simmonds says the brand has become a go-to for his ceramic tools. “Their range is every bit as luxurious as you would expect,” he says. This brush in particular helps seal the cuticle for an extra-smooth and shiny blowout, and can even work for extra-long hair. Have short hair? Try the 25mm version for the same luxury experience.

Bristle type: syntheticBarrel material: ceramic coatingSize options: 25mm–53mm

Like anything worth learning, styling your hair with one of the best round brushes takes practice. "Ask your stylist if they’ll do a lesson," offers Gamez. It also might be helpful to reference a YouTube or TikTok tutorial. The general guidance is: start fresh out of the shower, detangle your hair, and then begin blow-drying and brushing small sections of hair, working your way around your head. You can start in the front or back sections. Professional hair colorist Tylor Johnson recommends starting with the front sections around your hairline, as your hairline pieces are the most important and require the most precision, and you don't want to be fatigued when styling them.

What Are the Benefits of Using a Round Brush and Blow-Dryer?

It’s all about the shape. “The round brush allows the hair to wrap around it, creating a bend in the hair as it dries and cools,” explains Mohapi, who explains that a regular hairbrush should not be used for heat-styling. “Expensive brushes like Masons are not designed for direct heat, and you will ruin a perfectly beautiful brush,” says the stylist.

Several top stylists prefer to use round brushes and blow-dryers separately rather than an all-in-one device, as it enhances control while minimizing hair damage. "Blow-dry brushes tend to be high heat, so if you’re putting that tool on soaking wet hair, you’re going to overdry and damage it," explains Gamez. "A blow-dryer allows you to create space between the heat and the hair shaft as needed. You can control the air direction of a dryer with your nozzle, pointing downward and sealing the cuticle, resulting in shinier, smoother hair, as opposed to a hot brush that blows air outward in all directions."

On the other hand, blow-dry round brushes definitely have their benefits. According to Valles, one key factor is their overall ease of use to craft salon-worthy styles at home. "A blow-dry brush makes the process so much easier," Valles says. "Some people aren't as coordinated handling a brush and dryer simultaneously, but the blow-dry brush gives the same results and is easier to manage." At the end of the day, the best round brush for you is one you actually enjoy using regularly.

“Many people wouldn’t want to use a boar bristle brush for ethical reasons, and there are lots of good alternatives, so boar bristle isn’t the only option,” explains Mohapi. “It is, however, a highly effective material and is used extensively in hairbrush manufacturing globally.” Here’s why: “Boar bristles are highly tolerant of heat and won’t melt or buckle under the intense extremes of a packed Friday at the salon. The fibers are both strong and flexible and have the unique quality of being able to almost grab the hair and hold it in place as you pass it through the brush.”

That said, the stylist adds that some synthetics are nearly as good. They just may not last as long. “Nylon is a different thing altogether,” he notes. “Nylon will close down the cuticle and help create a shiny, slick finish to the hair. This is great for straight hair, but nylon brushes take all the body out of the hair, so when you use a nylon round brush, it has a different type of finish.”

#6826. I'm the Friend Everyone Texts for Tableware Recs. Here's Where I Send Them

Consider this your shortcut to a beautifully dressed table.

The art of tablescaping starts with some really beautiful tableware. No disrespect to placemats, but the glasses, plates, and cutlery that your guests use tend to take the spotlight. Everything tastes better coming out of a fun wine glass. And presentation is key. The right serving platter makes your weeknight leftovers look Michelin-starred. We know your design taste is as unique as your palate, so we wanted to make your shopping experience as seamless as possible. Below, we reveal over a dozen of the very best tableware brands on the market.

Whether you’re a frequent dinner party host or you’re romanticizing dinners for one, you deserve to find something truly unique for your dining table. We focused our search on dinnerware, glassware, cutlery, and serving essentials. (Table linens matter, but that’s a whole other article.) Of course, we started with the pieces filling our editors’ kitchens and dining rooms. That’s where we keep the cake plates for our favorite little treats, and high-quality wine glasses that aren’t too fragile for busy kitchens. But trust us, after going to the best design markets in the country and abroad, these are the brands that wow us year after year. They’re built to last and make every host’s life way easier.

East Fork was founded by Henri Matisse’s great-grandson, and the artistry shows in every piece. The hand-thrown pottery is crafted from regionally sourced clay in Asheville, North Carolina. The lightly speckled, colorful stoneware plates are a favorite of Associate Shopping Editor Janae McKenzie, especially the Matisse Cake Plate sets.

Fable is a tableware brand designed for the frequent family dinner host. The brand’s Canyon Clay collection brings a warm, sunbaked terracotta to their durable serveware. It’s dishwasher-safe and meant to hold up to your messiest meals, with a touch of elegance.

If you’re looking for the kind of beautiful plates and cups that can hold up to outdoor use, look no further than Thomas Fuchs’s melamine collection. These chip-proof, shatter-proof plates are designed for al fresco meals, and the half-and-half design adds some playful color.

We already loved Hommey for their viral striped cotton bedding, but the brand has brought that signature look to your tabletop. Even your cutlery deserves a little color-drenching, and certain colors are already selling out. It’s the kind of small upgrade that makes every meal feel a bit more special.

Let’s talk glassware: no one does clarity like Simon Pearce. It catches the light beautifully. Every single glassware piece from this brand is handmade by one of a few expert artisans, and the imperfection of handmade goods is celebrated here. (You can even get the extra quirky ones—called Seconds—for a discounted price.)

Chances are you’ve seen Serax tableware in a designer department store. The brand has decades of tabletop excellence under its belt, and its designer collaborations result in delightfully creative pieces. The Carte Blanche collection, designed to look like hand-drawn sketches, is one of our favorites.

Anthropologie is the home of little luxuries for everywhere in your home, but we’re especially partial to their small space-friendly tabletop pieces. Espresso cups and dessert plates just make breakfast feel way more glamorous. The gilded Bistro Tile collection is perfect for anyone wanting some Parisian flair in their kitchen.

Sabre is another iconic name in the world of tabletop, and one look at their stainless steel cutlery sets will tell you why. You can choose handles crafted from teak wood, tortoiseshell or a glossy lacquer, and you’ll love setting the table with them at dinnertime.

It really is the little things that make a difference, and at Mogutable, those are the irresistibly cute chopsticks rests. Imagine a few of these adorable food-shaped rests scattered across your dinner table, or letting each dinner guest select one of their own before noodles are served.

Arhaus is already one of our favorite furniture brands, so it’s no surprise the sophisticated home destination has incredible serveware. Their dinnerware sets get pretty pricey, so we suggest starting with their hosting essentials: napkin rings, coasters, and cocktail shakers that help you host like a pro.

Would you believe it if we told you those are paper plates? Table Two Studio is all about disposable plates for easy cleanup, without sacrificing style. The botanical patterns remind us of archival fabric prints from our favorite textile houses. Your guests will feel extra special, and you won’t spend hours doing dishes.

The Museum of Modern Art really knows how to curate an inspiring collection, and that extends to their home goods. It’s McKenzie’s first stop whenever she wants to find a cool hostess gift. Their glassware is unmatched, especially for any fans of colored glass or playful motifs.

Fishs Eddy dinnerware pays homage to a symbol of Americana and hospitality: the diner. Their green-band dishes, checkered plates, and simple white mugs are all evocative of a steaming diner breakfast after a long night. All of that cozy nostalgia warms up your family meals.

If you love all things spiraled and swirly, this is the glassware brand for you. The delicate pops of color and squiggled stems on the glass pitchers make even water look like a fun beverage. Perfect for cocktail hour, and sure to be treasured.

If you want to spot an Ichendorf Milano glass in the wild, look for colorful motifs from nature. A stem shaped like Lily of the Valley flowers or a fish at the bottom of a tumbler are telltale signs. It’s the kind of glassware you’d love to put on your registry and display.

Jono Pandolfi plates are a fine dining staple. They’re served at luxury restaurants across the country—you can even see them listed on the page of each glaze color. So if you like to get experimental in the kitchen and want your dishes to look Michelin star, you can literally use what the pros use.

The brand that ushered in a ceramic cookware takeover also has beautiful ceramic tableware. They have dinnerware bundles that are easy to build on over time, perfect for cozy brunches even in tiny apartment kitchens to feel a little fancier. It’s a starter set that doesn’t feel like it’s from the bargain bin.

#6818. The Complicated Politics of Forgiving a Pop Star

Slayyyter’s Grammy moment is raising bigger questions about what accountability really looks like.

It’s official: Slayyyter has graduated from being a gay guy music video night staple to a full-on Grammy nominee since dropping her first major-label album, WORST GIRL IN AMERICA.

The underdog has seemingly become a Main Pop Girl before our very eyes. She drew a record-breaking 50,000 people at Lollapalooza and her first-ever headlining show at Madison Square Garden sold out in minutes. Her viral hit, “CRANK,” even landed a perfectly placed needle-drop in the equally awards-buzzy film Club Kid. These are only a fraction of the feats that prove her Best New Artist nod has been years in the making and, dare I say, rightfully earned.

But what happens when the pains of her past finally catch up to her ascent? At the height of her breakout year, old social media posts using racial slurs resurfaced and introduced new fans to her regrettable history just as she was building momentum for the Grammys.

Though she had previously taken accountability for her behavior in 2020, she seemingly surrendered to the recent “Slayyyter Is Over Party” conversations fueling Stan Twitter with a post on her private account.

“This newfound attention is cool and all, but it’s unsettling about having people try to find things to ruin me with,” she reportedly wrote. “Why do you want to see me fail so bad?”

Slayyyter isn’t the first pop star to have old social media behavior resurface as her career reaches new heights. Ethel Cain penned a four-page statement acknowledging her racist posts after the controversy surrounding them escalated, including the doxxing of her family and partner. When Camila Cabello’s use of the N-word resurfaced in 2021, she publicly apologized and took part in weekly racial healing sessions. While the circumstances aren’t identical, each situation raises questions about what accountability looks like when a pop star’s past follows them into a new chapter.

“Everyone has to be online to build their brand and promote their work in 2026, but a lot of times, the parasocial relationship that leads to monumental disappointment for some fans when they find out their fave isn't perfect is due to their being online,” comedian and podcast host Akilah Hughes, who called Slayyyter out for her recent response, tells Cosmopolitan.

Hughes only discovered the artist this past year after hearing her songs in several New Music Friday roundups. She admits that she “was definitely paying attention” to each drop and “was ready to be ‘early’ on” her music until those old posts popped up on her social feeds.

“I don’t think Slayyyter is necessarily a victim, though it’s definitely unfortunate that—as she’s put out this great album and is in the midst of a huge ascent—her own behavior is haunting her,” she explains.

For Kiara Smith, a culture writer and podcast host who has followed Slayyyter since 2019, her posts brought a different kind of disappointment. At first, she was skeptical about the scandal’s reemergence.

“It seemed like someone just resurfaced the posts to start drama, rather than actually caring about what she said,” Smith tells Cosmopolitan.

In the heat of the hate train, users allegedly started piling on fabricated screenshots that falsely depicted her as targeting Black recording artist Normani. Smith acknowledges that while she should be held accountable for the real posts, the fake ones serve no additional purpose.

“What she said was terrible and inexcusable, but we have to be accurate when sharing things so as not to cause further harm to Black people for no reason,” Smith shares. “Black trauma is not a hot-button topic to debate. Tweets like that hurt real people and should be treated seriously.”

Hughes echoes a similar sentiment. “For a lot of young people, being the perfection police is a way to gain clout online,” she says. “Having an encyclopedic knowledge of the times everyone has messed up is akin to power.”

She continues, “Offline, in the real world, people are much more willing to give grace and additional chances. Because real life affords nuance, and the internet affords none.

“I think the internet is a very unforgiving place and has no place for nuance,” Smith adds. “If a person fucks up in real life, you can not fuck with them all you want. But in the end, I think everyone should want that person to be better and learn from their mistakes.”

But is that grace extended equally? Can an artist—particularly a female artist—ever really overcome her past mistakes?

Slayyyter did her part years ago, yet she’s forced to revisit her past and educate herself as she reaches new levels of fame. Male counterparts like Chris Brown and Morgan Wallen have historically been awarded second (and third…and fourth) chances for other serious offenses, yet have continued to enjoy commercial success and industry support.

“We know that the internet and the world are way less forgiving to women. If you’re a woman, you have to do 10 times more to come back from a controversy,” Smith says. “Lots of people have had controversies where they say a lot of nothing, but when this first came out years ago, Slayyyter seemed genuinely apologetic and even donated to many Black people and organizations afterwards. Not to say that excuses it, but we have to consider the effort she’s already made.”

Two things can be true: while pop stars should be held accountable for such offenses, they should also be able to reflect and move forward. This isn’t to defend or endorse their actions, but to invite fans and artists to have more productive conversations—especially as this vicious cycle of scarlet letter-stamping continues to be normalized, particularly with female acts.

Accountability doesn’t guarantee forgiveness, nor should it. But that doesn’t mean any possibility of growth should be off the table, and Slayyyter has seemingly come to peace with that.

When describing her personal thesis behind WORST GIRL IN AMERICA to Cosmopolitan back in March, she nearly prophesied what her come-up would inevitably entail.

“You can’t really escape where you’re from,” she said. “You can change and grow into something better, but the things that happen to you when you’re young—you carry them with you for the rest of your life.”

#6814. Doctors Have Allegedly Told Maura Higgins She Needs to “Drop Out” of ‘Dancing With the Stars’

Here’s the latest on whether or not Maura will be dancing this week.

This has been an especially intense season of Dancing with the Stars for mannnny reasons, and now fan-favorite Maura Higgins is battling a serious rib injury behind the scenes. While Maura confirmed last week that she was feeling a lot better, The Sun reports that she’s still in pain—and doctors have told her the only way to heal is to stop dancing.

“Maura was told by the doctors that there’s nothing they can do and nothing that will get better or feel better until she’s done dancing,” a source tells the outlet. “She would have to drop out of the show to fix the problems in her ribs, which she refuses to do. She’s not quitting this far into it and made that clear. So she’s going to just work through the agony for the next week, hopefully two weeks, and deal with her body and health issues after.”

The outlet also reports that DWTS pro Mark Ballas has adjusted choreography to help Maura out, and that she’s postponed several rehearsals going into the semifinals in order to rest up. That said, she was pictured at rehearsals on November 15, so it looks like she’ll be dancing on Tuesday night:

Meanwhile, Maura confirmed after Grammy Night that X-rays showed “no crack” in her rib, but “like any muscle damage or cartilage damage, it would feel the exact same, and it’s the same treatment. You kind of just have to let it heal.”

Dancing With the Stars airs its semifinals live on Tuesday night, November 17, and Maura and Mark will be performing a samba to “Samba Vocalizado” by Luciano Perrone and a Viennese waltz to “Shrike” by Hozier.

#6798. 12 12 12 Shakes Up the Tired Heist Formula with One Great Idea

Can the Anthony Mackie-led crime drama pull it off?

“A high-asset robbery, professionally conceived, is twelve months in planning, twelve hours in execution,” says Anthony Mackie’s character, FBI special agent Jack Strickland, at the start of the new Apple TV crime drama, 12 12 12. “A bank robber who is not caught within twelve days is not caught at all.”

It’s an amazing premise—honestly, one of the best I’ve heard in a long time. The first episode takes the viewer one month into planning the heist, one day into pulling off the heist, and one hour into their attempted escape. Then, episode 2 will move along to two months, two days, and two hours. Neat, right? But the big question for cocreators Jim Keeble and Dudi Appleton’s time-shifting heist series remains the same as any seemingly impossible bank job: Do they have the right team to pull it off?

From the two-episode premiere—now available to stream on Apple TV—I’d say we’re off to a very strong start. 12 12 12’s opening scene pays homage to Ocean’s 11’s Shaobo Qin when a contortionist unfolds his body and climbs out of a large safety deposit box. It’s one of the most genius ideas I’ve seen in a heist story of the last few years—I mean, talk about a man on the inside—especially in an overcrowded field of complex heist series. Remember Netflix’s Kaleidoscope, which allowed viewers to watch the story starting on any episode in the series? Well, 12 12 12 is far more than just a gimmick.

The story follows FBI special agent Jack Strickland (Mackie), who has tracked down one of the world’s best bank robbers, Paul Galvin (Jamie Dornan), to Zurich, Switzerland. Galvin and his unruly brother Bobby (Jack Kesy, who was last seen playing Robbie Pedergrast’s late brother on Task), made an enemy of Strickland when the FBI agent’s partner was killed as she attempted to arrest them during a major heist in Newark, New Jersey.

Now, Strickland has a personal vendetta against the man. He throws his philosophy that you’ll never catch a bank robber after twelve days of the robbery out the window—which is fine because the creators fabricated the whole “12 12 12” FBI rule anyway just to provide episodic framing for the series. (Maybe it resets now that Galvin is planning another heist?) Either way, 12 12 12 does a great job of making sure you never get lost in the three timelines.

Strickland’s white whale is also far from a one-note character himself. He’s a man following in his father’s footsteps to get out of a life of poverty, which would be easier if his brother Bobby wasn’t such of a wild card who can’t be trusted unattended for a single second.

As it turns out, Bobby gambled away all their money from the Newark heist, including Paul’s half. So his brother’s debtors force Paul back into a life of crime under threat of violence to his new lady love in France, his father-in-law, and their unborn child. It’s also quickly revealed that our contortionist is just one of the interesting members of Paul’s team, who are all tasked by Bobby’s mysterious benefactors with robbing a Zurich bank of 800,000 Swiss francs all owned by the Russian mafia.

From the first two episodes released so far, the 12 12 12 team seems like it has the right men for the job. The timeline-shifting format allows the series to do much more than simply break the action to provide a little backstory. As we move from months ahead to days after, and vice-versa, we also switch perspectives that allow for Paul and Jack to trade who is the hero and who is the villain.

Jack can be both the righteous FBI agent pursuing the bank robbers in the present and a man clearly far too hellbent on revenge in the past. The same goes for Paul, whose team blackmails, honeypots, and occasionally murders throughout the heist, while his backstory paints him as a good man who was forced down a dark road.

Where 12 12 12 falls short, at least so far, is in the script. Kari Skogland (who previously worked with Mackie on The Falcon and the Winter Soldier) directs the two-part premiere handily enough, but Keeble and Appleton’s writing is very formulaic. Scripts don’t always matter when you’re working with amazing characters like the contortionist lockpick. True Detective: Night Country’s Kali Reis is also on the team. Her mission is to hack into the subway system so that they can make their escape on public transit, which, of course, goes wrong. But when you have so much dialogue doubling as exposition in between the action, it feels like everyone’s conversations are more for the viewer than they are for each other.

For now, I’m hopeful that all the kinks are worked out once we move past the who, what, and why of 12 12 12’s opening episodes. Given the gimmick, and the fact that the series is only eight episodes instead of twelve, I’m excited to see just what throws the entire concept of the series into chaos.

#6794. Design Experts Reveal When It’s Actually OK to Start Decorating for the Holidays

There’s no hard rule—but your HOA may have one.

Now that the Halloween candy bowls are empty, you might find thoughts of tinsel, garland, and fairy lights dancing in your head, but is it really time to trade your pumpkins for holiday trimmings? Some have strong opinions on the topic, half believing the jolly spirit of the holiday season doesn't start until after Thanksgiving, the other rushing to unbox their silver bells as soon as the clock strikes 12 on November 1—but the choice isn’t always yours to make.

If you belong to a Homeowners’ Association (and over 78.1 million Americans do), the question of when to put up your outdoor décor may be dictated by their guidelines. HOAs tend to have rules about holiday decorations, restrictions covering their location (within the boundaries of your yard, of course), sound level (not too loud), safety (no open flames), light color, and when the decorations can be installed. In some communities, you can even be fined for bringing out the twinkle lights and nativity creche before Thanksgiving—this happened to one Florida man in 2002, when he was hit with a fine of $1,000 for putting his lights up on November 6.

If you don’t live in an HOA, you may not get fined for decorating too early, but you will get judged by neighbors who find pre-Thanksgiving holiday displays a little, well, tacky. To be fair, holding until after Thanksgiving does have its perks—why would you pass on the opportunity to decorate a gorgeous fall tablescape? In the spirit of peace on earth and goodwill toward man, we asked design experts what to do. Here’s what they said.

Additional copy by Meghan Shouse and Alyssa Khan.

You might follow a religious tradition that guides your holiday decorating, like placing the Baby Jesus in the nativity on Christmas Day or keeping the tree up until Epiphany. But when it comes to secular etiquette, there are no hard-and-fast rules about when to start (or stop) decking the halls.

“Most people find it tasteful to stick within the weeks or the month of the holiday itself,” points out Lizzie Post, co-president of The Emily Post Institute and author of Emily Post’s Etiquette—The Centennial Edition. “That said, there is no etiquette that dictates it. It’s the same way you can paint your house neon orange with blue stripes—there’s nothing that says you can’t. It’s really, do you want to be the person who does?”

For designer Tan France, the answer to that question is decidedly yes. For the past decade, he and his husband have been decorating their tree on Halloween and taking it down on Valentine’s Day. “I’m British, and we don’t really do Halloween,” he explains. “When I moved here 16 years ago, I was more obsessed with Christmas. So on Halloween day, when everyone else is crafting their costumes, I want to be decorating the tree. And a tree brings my husband and me a lot of joy when we come down and turn the lights on first thing in the morning. It’s just a lovely thing to perk your day up.”

Do people question France about his decision to put up decorations so early, and for so long? “Oh gosh, yeah, everyone asks that,” he says. “Online, the press, neighbors. And my answer is really simple: Why delay joy?”

If you are jonesing to do some holiday decorating, there's good news: While Christmas might be weeks away, there is one happening right now. It’s called Thanksgiving.

“Anytime before midnight of Thanksgiving is too early to decorate,” insists Wendy Kerrigan, founder of Atelier Design in New Orleans. “Filling the house with Christmas decorations too soon really takes away from the beauty of Thanksgiving, moving your mind into the bustle of Christmas.”

Kerrigan wishes more people would celebrate the fall season for longer; rushing to the tinsel takes away from the beauty of the autumn season, “Thanksgiving can be experienced in a special way too, filling the house with fall colors, fall leaves, gourds, and cornucopia,” says Kerrigan. “Instead of trying to hurry the year along, enjoy each family celebration for what it is.”

Even more, sprinting to the Yuletide season robs the holiday of its own magic. “What makes things special when they’re once a year is that they’re experiences you look forward to,” says Vermont-based interior designer Joshua Smith. “How many holidays can we take at one time? Are we doing Black Friday in July now? Let’s just enjoy the season that we’re in!”

When it comes to holiday decorating, many hands make light work. Several designers say they take advantage of the long holiday weekend and enforced family time to rope in some extra help making spirits bright. “In my parents' home, we start decorating right after Thanksgiving dinner. It’s a rare opportunity for everyone to be together, and my siblings and I stay to help out our parents,” says New York-based designer Mikel Welch. “Plus, it’s a much better tradition than terrorizing sales associates on Black Friday Eve!"

Smith has a plan for bringing the Christmas spirit to his home, without shouldering the burden of doing all the hall-decking himself. Last year, his family decorated their trees over Thanksgiving weekend and made it a tree-decorating contest. With his husband's family in town, “they’ll be divided into three teams, decorating three trees, and I’ll go around at the end judging, and everyone will win prizes. We’re not all going to be together at Christmas, so this activity is a fun way to celebrate the season and not do all the work myself,” he says.

Still, early decorators would argue that the amount of effort needed to set up holiday decor is another reason to put up decorations early—and keep them up late. “This is the most miserable time of the year; it gets dark so early, it’s so darn cold. I just think you should do everything you can to bring yourself joy,” says France. “Why put in all that effort for a few weeks?”

Even those who are normally on the side of waiting to decorate agree that desperate times call for dopamine decorating. Whether it’s the state of your inbox or the state of the world that is stressing you out, if you’re feeling a little blue, go right ahead and pull out the red and green.

“In a perfect calendar, the day after Thanksgiving would be my official start to the holiday season—it's traditionally the day when my family goes out into the woods of Tahoe National Forest to chop down our tree, drag it home, and decorate it,” says designer Noz Nozawa of Noz Design. “But this year, the day after Halloween is when I let the holidays start—there's plenty to worry about, and having my nutcrackers and string lights out a few weeks early can only boost spirits.”

Whenever you decorate, it can be a way to bring fun to a not-always joyful world. “We have no control over a lot of things in our lives, but when we walk through the door of our home, we choose to focus our energy and attention on whether that is on fear or on love and joy,” says Smith. “And this holiday season, I think more than ever we have to focus on ways to bring more light to ourselves and to each other.”

When it comes to early decor, “I can handle it from my neighbor; I cannot handle it from a corporation,” says Post. That may be because we know the Christmas displays popping up in a big-box store alongside the Halloween candy are trying to sell us something, while a neighbor decorating is spreading joy, spending their own time and money to brighten the dark winter nights. For a shop, holiday decorating is mercenary. For an individual, it’s a mitzvah.

If you appreciate the joy inspired by holiday displays and do want to start setting them up early or keep them up late, consider finding a middle ground by leaning toward decor that’s more about lighting up the night, rather than marking a particular occasion. “I see a lot of people in Vermont leaving the actual lights up, not so much like blow-up Santas or things like that, but the actual lights up for the entire winter,” says Post.

If you’re a minimalist (or live in a studio apartment), you don’t need a tree to decorate. “If you don’t have a tree, I’m going to invite you to place a hurricane lamp on your coffee table with a wreath of greenery around it and light a candle in the evening,” says Smith. “Because what we know from neuroscience and neuroaesthetics is, you’ve got to engage the senses to nourish the soul!” Light a candle or turn on some twinkle lights, and you’re liable to spark joy as well.

France would argue that even his trees aren’t occasion-specific. “Here’s the thing: I’m Muslim, and my tree and the lights on our house aren’t necessarily for Christmas,” he explains. “It’s to celebrate Thanksgiving, Christmas, and the New Year. This is the most miserable time of the year. It gets dark so early, it gets light so late, it’s so darn cold. I just think do everything you can to bring yourself joy.”

Though you’d never find them rushing to hang their stockings, the designer duo behind the Kansas City-based firm Kobel + Co, Elizabeth Bennett and Mallory Robins, say there’s one design scheme you can always tap into if you find yourself in the class of outliers running to get decor up before Christmas Eve. “Ralph Lauren Holiday isn't just having a moment; it's a classic holiday go-to for so many reasons,” Bennett explains. “Warm crimsons, rich browns, tactile fabrics, and blue-green plaids make a beautiful holiday palette. Layer in antique silver pieces and simple glass ornaments. A little patina and variation keep everything from feeling too matched. The goal is a warm, welcoming home with pieces that feel collected and personal.” After all, you want it to feel like home, even at Christmas.

#6793. It’s Confirmed: Robert Pattinson Is the Next James Bond

Hardly a day goes by without a report dropping that someone’s cousin’s friend’s brother overheard someone else’s cousin’s friend’s brother at a bar say that [insert hot British actor here] is the next James Bond. And up until this morning, Amazon MGM and director Denis Villeneuve have not confirmed one. single. thing. But it’s official: Robert Pattinson is the new James Bond.

The rumor mill has been churning for months. Several actors screen-tested for the iconic role, including Jack Lowden, Josh O’Connor, but Robert Pattinson was an eleventh hour surprise. Matthew Belloni wrote in Puck News last week that there was reason to think Robert is favored to become the next 007.

Turns out all potential Bonds sign contracts for the film and its follow-ups—and three separate sources told Belloni that while Jack and Josh “were required to sign up for four Bonds,” Robert “was able to lower that commitment to three” and negotiate himself “a whole lot more money than the others.”

More sources explained that since Robert’s “test deal was so difficult to close,” people close to the situation were “wondering why the filmmakers would bother if they didn’t really want him.”

There’s been so much discourse about whether or not Bond should be an established household name (traditionally, he isn’t), with former casting director Debbie McWilliams—who cast the last three Bonds—telling The Independent, “It is absolutely essential that he retains a total enigma. I don’t want to see any of them as Bond because we now know so much about them. We want to know as little about them personally as possible, because that what spies are. We don’t need to know where he goes shopping or who his parents are, or where he lives. We never want to see him at home. And a vital element of the whole thing is his job description. He’s licensed to kill, and we have to believe that he can do that. If you don’t, then you’ve lost the audience.”

Casting Robert Pattinson definitely challenges that approach, but we’ll see how it goes!

#6787. Meet the Real-Life Partners of the ‘Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping’ Cast

The new cast is stacked—and sadly, most of them are taken IRL.

At long last, The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping is finally (almost) here, revisiting the world of Panem 24 years before the events of the original Hunger Games trilogy (and 40 years after The Ballad of Songbirds and Snakes). Centered around a young Haymitch Abernathy, Sunrise on the Reaping tells the story of the Second Quarter Quell (aka the 50th Hunger Games), during which the Capitol doubles the stakes by demanding twice the normal number of tributes.

Of course, that means the cast is twice as packed with familiar faces (including Cosmo’s latest cover stars Whitney Peak, Mckenna Grace, and Maya Hawke). Wondering who from the star-studded cast is taken IRL? Keep scrolling to meet the real-life partners of The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping cast, from Joseph Zada’s lowkey girlfriend Polly Talarico to Mckenna Grace’s adorable showmance with Mason Thames.

Current relationship status: Dating Polly Talarico

While Joseph keeps his love life under the radar, he’s (reportedly) been dating “student and nonactor” Polly Talarico since meeting her “in a bar in Sydney earlier this year,” as he revealed during a Vanity Fair interview.

Current relationship status: Married to Jazz Charton

Currently relationship status: Dating Sophia Rose McDowell

Current relationship status: Married to Laura Louie

Perhaps one of the chillest relationships in Hollywood, Woody’s romance with Laura Louie began when she was hired as his personal assistant back in 1987, though it took him years to confess how he really felt. They welcomed three daughters together before finally saying “I do” in 2008.

#6786. Safe Word: The Best Jewelry Launches of November 2026

Safe Word is a series highlighting the latest trends in the jewelry market, keeping you up to date on all things that sparkle and shine.

November’s jewelry launches are looking both backward and forward, with designers revisiting their roots, reworking archival ideas, and stepping into entirely new territory. First up, Quentin Jones is taking her surrealist art off the wall and onto the body with ‘Nocturne’, her first jewelry collection; Pomellato reaches back to 1997 to revive “Moneta Orientale” as a colorful modern talisman; David Yurman celebrates the magic of a New York holiday with Michael B. Jordan, Yerin Ha, and Inde Navarrette; Vhernier revisits its iconic “Trottola” design with new proportions and contrasting materials; Di Volpe reinterprets the classic tennis bracelet with customizable gemstones in “DiSegno”; Brent Neale transforms the classic bow into sculptural fine jewelry with “Wrap It Up”; and Kendra Scott launches the new “Kendra Scott Collection,” reimagining her signature Spike motif as a personal talisman. From archival revivals and meaningful motifs to innovative designs and new creative directions, November’s launches prove that sometimes the best way forward starts with looking back. For the rest of the month, we’ve got you covered on all the jewelry-related happenings worth knowing about.

Quentin Jones is taking her art off the wall and onto the body with “Nocturne,” the first jewelry collection from House of Quentin Jones. Following the multidisciplinary artist’s debut of surrealist ceramics and tapestries last year, the new collection transforms recurring gestures and symbols from her work into sculptural collars, cuffs, armbands, pendants, chains, and earrings. Eyes, hands, stars, moons, tears, and knots take shape in silver and gold vermeil alongside freshwater pearls, onyx, opals, jade, glass, and silk tassels, giving the pieces a dreamlike, talismanic quality. Beginning as drawings and hand collages in Jones’s Brooklyn studio, each design carries her surrealist visual language into wearable form, further blurring the line between art, design, and fashion.

Kendra Scott Launches an Eponymous Collection

Kendra Scott is offering a more personal look into her design world with the launch of the “Kendra Scott Collection,” a new line spanning fashion and fine jewelry. Its first edition, “The Spike,” revisits a silhouette the designer has worn throughout her career as a personal symbol of strength, confidence, and conviction. Inspired by nature and the radiance of the night sky, the collection translates the pointed motif into sculptural necklaces, earrings, bracelets, and charms, balancing bold proportions with intricate craftsmanship. Fashion jewelry incorporates tiger’s eye, malachite, and turquoise, while fine jewelry introduces lab-grown diamonds, pavé detailing, labradorite, and lapis. From statement-making links to delicate diamond charms, the collection offers a new interpretation of Scott’s longtime signature, inviting wearers to make the symbol their own.

Alison Lou’s ‘New Wave’ Collection Experiments With New Materials

Alison Lou is entering a new chapter with “New Wave,” a fine jewelry collection that introduces unexpected materials and a fresh approach to the designer’s signature playful aesthetic. Exploring the relationship between movement and form, the collection pairs lightweight titanium and ceramic with lab-grown diamonds, creating fluid, sculptural silhouettes that curve and catch the light. Available in Fig and Denim Blue colorways, the designs include wavy rings, diamond-accented signets, delicate thread necklaces, and statement drop earrings. With its unconventional materials and easy-to-wear designs, “New Wave” brings a new sense of movement to Alison Lou’s everyday jewelry.

Jenny Bird and Emese Gormley Partner on the Anyu Ring

Jenny Bird is expanding her JB FINE collection with a new collaboration featuring lifestyle creator and Lipstick on the Rim podcast host Emese Gormley. Named in honor of Gormley’s mother, the Anyu Ring offers a personal take on the classic signet, featuring three lab-grown baguette diamonds set in 14-karat yellow or white gold. The understated design combines the timeless appeal of a signet ring with a sentimental tribute to family, bringing a new perspective to Bird’s growing fine jewelry offering. Available exclusively on Jenny Bird’s website, the collaboration makes a case for jewelry that feels as meaningful as it looks.

Pomellato Revives Its 1997 Moneta Orientale Pendant as a Colorful Talisman

Pomellato is looking to its archives for its latest good-luck charm. For the newest chapter of “Memorie d’Archivio,” the maison revives Moneta Orientale, a 1997 pendant inspired by the ancient symbolism of a circle surrounding a square, representing the harmony between heaven and earth. Made in 33 stone-and-pavé combinations, the design pairs colorful hard stones including black jade, amazonite, sodalite, rhodochrosite, and labradorite with Pomellato’s signature irregular pavé of diamonds, sapphires, rubies, and more. Offered in two proportions, each pendant transforms the archival motif into a modern talisman for prosperity and protection.

Bibi van der Velden Makes Her High Jewelry Debut With ‘Eclipse’

Bibi van der Velden is entering a new era with “Eclipse,” the designer’s first high jewelry collection and most refined exploration of diamonds to date. Inspired by the fleeting moment when light gives way to darkness, the collection includes two six-piece suites: one in white diamonds and another defined by an ombré of diamonds into gray spinels deepening into black. Van der Velden’s signature alligator motif is abstracted into fluid, sculptural silhouettes, with scales engraved into the white gold on the reverse of each piece. “With Eclipse, I wanted to take those familiar elements and transform them into something more abstract and sculptural,” she says. “It’s a collection about transformation, which has always been at the heart of my work.”

Aflalo and Leandra Medine Cohen Turn Everyday Indulgences Into Fine Jewelry

Aflalo is finding the humor in our obsession with wellness with “The Vanities,” a new two-piece fine jewelry capsule designed exclusively by Leandra Medine Cohen. Playing with the contradiction between longevity and life’s little indulgences, the collection transforms two unlikely everyday accessories into precious objects. The Memento Mori is a 14-karat yellow gold cigar band set with more than four carats of diamonds, designed to slip over an Oura Ring, while The Libertine is a sterling silver pendant case adorned with diamonds and a wild-haired woman outlined in gold, designed to hold nicotine pouches or other small essentials. Equal parts extravagant and irreverent, the collection makes a playful case for enjoying life’s pleasures without sacrificing personal style.

Julia Berolzheimer’s Hart Jewelry Collection Draws on Childhood Memories

Hart is teaming up with Julia Berolzheimer for a new jewelry collection that celebrates the art of the stack. Inspired by Berolzheimer’s childhood memories of her grandmother’s California ranch and her collection of Art Deco minaudières, the collaboration brings together whimsical charms, colorful gemstones, and meaningful talismans designed to be mixed, layered, and restyled. Botanical motifs take shape in Tree of Life coins, daisies, bees, and fruit-inspired charms, while carnelian, turquoise, and pink opal add playful pops of color. One standout is the Perfume Vesta Case, a modern interpretation of a Victorian-era accessory that can hold a sample-sized fragrance and hang alongside other charms. With interchangeable chains, cords, and pendants, the collection makes a case for jewelry that feels just as personal as the memories behind it.

David Yurman Captures the Magic of a New York Holiday

David Yurman is celebrating the magic of the holidays in New York City with a new campaign featuring global brand ambassador Michael B. Jordan alongside actresses Yerin Ha and Inde Navarrette. Set against a snow-covered Washington Square Park, the campaign captures a series of quintessential New York moments, from Jordan and Ha facing off in a spirited card game to Navarrette hailing a cab in search of the perfect holiday gift. Throughout the campaign, the cast wears some of the house’s most recognizable designs, including 18-karat gold Cable bracelets and pieces from the “Starburst” and “Armory” collections. Directed by Bradford Young and photographed by Drew Vickers, the campaign brings together the spirit of the city, the joy of togetherness, and the timeless appeal of David Yurman’s jewelry.

Vhernier Reintroduces Its Iconic ‘Trottola’ Design With New Proportions

Vhernier is revisiting one of its most recognizable designs with a new interpretation of “Trottola,” the sculptural collection originally introduced in 2003. Inspired by the continuous motion of a spinning top, the latest designs play with proportions, contrasting materials, and asymmetrical shapes across rings, bracelets, necklaces, and earrings. Polished rose gold meets glossy black jet, with select pieces featuring diamond pavé for an added contrast of light and dark. The collection also focuses on versatility, from rings designed for stacking to convertible earclips that can be worn as studs or dangling earrings. With its fluid forms and unexpected material combinations, “Trottola” brings a fresh perspective to Vhernier’s signature sculptural approach to jewelry.

Di Volpe is rethinking the classic tennis bracelet with “DiSegno,” a new customizable fine jewelry system designed to change with its wearer. Using concealed, patent-pending connections, individual cultured diamonds and gemstones, called Elements, can be removed, rearranged, or added by hand, allowing wearers to transform bracelets into necklaces or earrings without compromising the seamless look of traditional tennis jewelry. The collection launches with 28 stone-and-cut combinations, including rubies, emeralds, sapphires, and diamonds, alongside a limited-edition collaboration with Lebanese artist Ali Cha’aban featuring five sculptural designs. With new cuts, colors, and artist collaborations planned for future releases, “DiSegno” makes the case for fine jewelry that’s never quite finished.

Brent Neale Riffs on the Bow in Fine Jewelry

Brent Neale is putting a bow on the holiday season with “Wrap It Up,” a new fine jewelry collection that transforms the familiar ribbon motif into sculptural works of art. Drawing inspiration from Old Hollywood glamour and the femininity of the 1960s, the designer plays with the contrast between rigid precious metals and the soft movement of fabric, crafting bows that twist, curl, and unfurl in 18-karat gold and platinum. Colored gemstones sit nestled within the ribbon-like forms, while pavé diamonds accentuate every curve and fold. Equal parts playful and precious, the collection brings a little gift-wrapped glamour to the jewelry box.

Grace Winfrey’s ‘Poppy Fortuna’ Collection Reimagines Good-Luck Charms

Grace Winfrey is bringing a little extra luck to the jewelry box with “Poppy Fortuna,” a new fine jewelry collection inspired by the natural beauty of the Italian landscape and symbols of good fortune. Scarlet poppies, sunflowers, bees, four-leaf clovers, horseshoes, and ladybugs come to life through vibrant gemstones and intricate pavé, creating miniature mosaics of color and texture. Highlights include a Lucky Bangle combining three good-luck charms, sculptural sunflower earrings with tiny ladybugs tucked into their petals, and layered charm necklaces designed to feel like modern keepsakes. Handcrafted in New York City, each piece transforms familiar symbols of prosperity, protection, and happiness into playful, colorful works of art.

Robinson Pelham Channels ’90s Glamour for Its Anniversary

Robinson Pelham is closing out its 30th anniversary celebrations with “Suite 96,” a new campaign and collection launch inspired by the glamour of late-’90s London. Set inside a private suite at St. James’s Hotel & Club in Mayfair, the campaign considers jewelry the ultimate room service, with diamonds arriving on trays, gold spilling out of wardrobes, and plenty of pieces to layer before a night out. At the center is “Studio 30,” a four-piece anniversary collection featuring diamond necklaces and statement earrings, including the sculptural Via and Hira necklaces, cascading diamond drops, and the bold Solstice Supreme. Alongside the anniversary designs, the house introduces new collections and additions to existing favorites, from chunky gold links and graphic heart rings to colorful enamel and gemstone combinations. Together, the launches celebrate three decades of Robinson Pelham’s bold, playful approach to fine jewelry.

Le Vian and Jared Revive Georgian-Era Romance

Le Vian is bringing a little old-world romance to Jared Jewelers with its new exclusive “Georgian Chocolate & Nude Diamonds” collection. Inspired by the candlelit jewels of the Georgian era, when jewelry was designed to capture and reflect the warmth of natural light, the collection rethinks historic buttercup settings in polished Honey Gold, with eight rounded prongs framing each natural diamond. The designs pair Le Vian’s signature Chocolate and Nude Diamonds with warm gold detailing across solitaire rings, anniversary bands, tennis bracelets, necklaces, and earrings. Select styles introduce colorful gemstones including Chocolate Quartz, Green Apple Peridot, and Grape Amethyst, giving the vintage-inspired silhouettes a contemporary update. With its sculptural settings and warm-toned stones, the collection brings the romance of antique jewelry into the present.

Wempe’s ‘Au:thentic’ Celebrates the Art of Goldsmithing

Wempe’s new gender-inclusive fine jewelry collection, “Au:thentic,” puts craftsmanship front and center, finding inspiration in the foundational skills taught to aspiring goldsmiths. The collection takes its shape from the jump ring, one of the first techniques Patrick Sieroslawski learned as an apprentice more than 22 years ago, translating its form to necklaces, bracelets, rings, earrings, and pendants. Handcrafted at Wempe’s atelier in Germany, the pieces come in 18-karat yellow, white, and rose gold, with select styles set with diamonds. “Au:thentic” also marks the beginning of a multiyear rollout, with three additional chapters set to expand on its design language through twists, wraps, and interlocking links.

#6784. These 9 Best Waxed Canvas Jackets Are Good-Looking and Hard-Wearing

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Nothing is better suited for cold, wet weather.

Why did we ever stray from this path? A good waxed canvas jacket will handle rough weather, sure—any new-age rain jacket can do that as well. But the magic of this cloth is the look. Made from heavy-duty cotton canvas treated with an oil-based waxed coating, these jackets offer a barrier against rain and snow while also blocking the wind—but they look damn good doing it.

Once you have a waxed canvas jacket, it becomes uniquely yours, developing a weathered patina with every wear. And if you give it a fresh coat of wax every year or so, these jackets will stay waterproof and last for most, if not all, of your life.

Here, we’ve rounded up our favorite styles from the names you’d expect, like Barbour, Filson, and L.L. Bean, alongside a few brands you might not know yet. Ready to brave the unexpected fall and winter weather? With one of these jackets, you will be.

An enduring icon thanks to its tenacious popularity, the Barbour Beaufort waxed jacket boasts all the classic details created by Dame Margaret Barbour, including a color-contrast corduroy collar (which you can turn up and secure when it gets cold), paneled tartan lining, flapped bellow pockets, and a two-way front zip. It’s truly an evergreen piece that will never go out of style, and Style Director Jonathan Evans argues that if you’re going to invest in a waxed canvas jacket, it should be the Beaufort.

For a chic, elevated look (pretty hard to achieve in rainy weather), Tom Beckbe’s Tensaw is a great pick. The Alabama-based brand has that hunting tradition, but it doesn’t go full upland. It’s more elevated than that.

Since 1948, the Trialmaster jacket has been Belstaff's signature style, its origin going back to the Scottish Six Days Trial, a motorcycle competition that attracts riders worldwide. Developed to keep the competitors dry in tough weather conditions, it’s built with black waxed cotton and features a legacy slanted map pocket and an adjustable belt, so you can always find the perfect fit.

Made from Filson’s signature waxed cotton, this jacket has protected foresters and outdoorsmen from brambles and abrasion in the rain-soaked forests of the Pacific Northwest since the 1920s—talk about an endorsement. The hip-length cut allows for easy access to pockets or a tool belt, and this type of fit will also stop the jacket from riding up when you’re seated.

For a super-warm option, Flint and Tinder’s flannel-lined style ensures you won’t feel a shiver or a sprinkle. Tough, timeless, and made in the U.S.A., it’s constructed with a weather-resistant Martexin seven-ounce sailcloth, which, like selvage denim or finely tanned leather, will only get better with age. It’s more lightweight than denim or leather, but still substantial enough to feel durable. Take it from our endorsement of the jacket:

“It’s not even like a techy Arc’teryx shell you might want to be more careful with because of the weight. This is the kind of jacket you don’t think twice about wearing. You wear it in the rain, not worrying if it brushes against brick walls. You toss it in the backseat of your car—or, even better, the backseat of a side-by-side on a weekend in the woods. Over time—years, I mean—the fabric picks up creases. They don’t ruin it. They define it. It becomes yours.”

The first thing you’ll notice about this jacket is that it’s actually true to size and has a great fit. It leaves just enough room for layering without the risk of looking sloppy, and it’s slim enough without feeling too restrictive. We also loved how comfortable it is and like that the weathered fabric gives it a classic vintage appeal (without having to spend hours searching a secondhand shop).

TBC doesn’t just make damn good boots—the brand has perfected the durable, rugged, highly functional jacket that doesn’t sacrifice style for comfort. We like how the water-resistant finish only gets better with age and how the polyester sleeve lining both allows for ease of movement and prevents the sleeves from bunching up. Plus, it’s great for colder days thanks to the warm blanket flannel lining.

Tecovas keeps it cool, casual, and authentic with a waxed canvas ranch jacket that makes an everyday outfit look even better than it would sans jacket. Whether you’re mending fence posts or making a quick dash to the office or pub, this jacket will help muzzle the nip in the air and keep downpours at bay. It’s lightweight and less bulky than some of the other options on this list, and it does have soft brushed lining on the inside for some added warmth. The Western-inspired details also set it apart from the more classic styles.

L.L. Bean, the brand known for its rugged outdoor gear, does a cool, modern twist on a classic hunting coat. Working with the originator of waxed cotton, British Millerain, the company designed this option with an innovative fabric that eliminates the typical cleaning fuss that comes with this type of garment.

#6782. Dressing Like Your Boyfriend Is Actually Fine

Take more, give less in the relationship, ladies.

Is it so wrong to dress like your boyfriend? Clearly, the internet thinks so. Most recently, Bella Hadid has found herself at the center of this conversation, with people highlighting her various boyfriend eras like some sort of sartorial identity crisis. From the emo-adjacent vibes when she dated the singer, The Weeknd, to the cowboy-rodeo-chic aesthetic with Adan Banuelos, and now her hockey-game-attending, matching outfit moment with new man Nathan Lacy, Bella has seemingly been accused of basing her clothes after her romantic partners.

I personally think it’s a silly argument given that Bella Hadid is one of the most notable supermodels with an eccentric, well-documented high-fashion sense of style. She was awarded Lyst’s 2022 Most Powerful Dresser, and she famously styles much of her own day-to-day looks. So who cares if, during a particular season of her life, she wants to tap into the aesthetic of the man she’s dating?

The same argument has been made about Ariana Grande, Kim Kardashian, and, more recently (even by her own sisters, who do the same thing), Kourtney Kardashian. Ariana’s personal style has never particularly screamed fashion girl to me, and I’m a huge proponent of the idea that not everyone needs to be one anyway. Grande works with stylists to build her looks, so if on her off days she wants to dress in tandem with her beau, there’s truly nothing wrong with that.

Kim Kardashian, meanwhile, notoriously skyrocketed into the high-fashion space during her relationship with Kanye West. To this day, Kim gives style credits to Kanye’s eye for fashion and his introducing her to the world she so desperately wanted to be part of. Did she take what he taught her and expand upon it, eventually establishing herself as a fashion mogul in her own right? Yes. So technically in the long term, borrowing an aesthetic from her boyfriend then husband clearly worked out for her.

And while I personally wouldn’t wear Kourtney’s ultra-rock-girlfriend aesthetic, I actually think it's fun. After spending a decade being terrorized by Scott Disick (terrible boyfriend, excellent family friend to her sisters), I think it’s perfectly fine for my girl to enjoy someone so much she clearly feels safe enough to wrap herself in his sartorial aesthetic.

This idea that women are in the wrong for “adopting” their man’s fashion seems uninteresting to me. If anything, women should be stealing men’s aesthetics and making them their own more often. There's something cheeky about the whole thing, a small flip of patriarchy on its head. How often do we see the internet go viral over the invisible labor women put into the men they date? Making their style better, recommending the haircut that best frames their face, teaching them how to groom themselves. It’s always give, give, give. So why not take something too? Because the next video down my timeline after those labor of love, “girlfriend effect” ones is women complaining about men taking everything they gave in a relationship (from hair and clothing reccs) and bringing it to the next woman.

There's something rather liberating about having your own style to return to while allowing yourself to experiment with whatever your boyfriend of the moment is into. It’s like playing dress-up. You’re enjoying a man, his world, and even his wardrobe, without having to make any of it your permanent identity—simply trying it out like free samples of ice cream. And when he’s no longer serving you, you move on. Thank you, next, as Miss Grande says.

Because women are so often expected to do the emotional and personal-care work in relationships, it’s honestly fine to me seeing these celebrities lean into their boyfriends’ fashion sense. (Side note: Incredible that these men have a style at all—it can be sartorially bleak out there!) Maybe his hockey jerseys look good on you. Maybe his leather jackets are better than yours. Maybe you just like the way you look in a cowboy hat right now.

It isn’t hurting anyone. And if anything, the most radical part of all is letting the men, for once, shape the fashion in their relationship. High time we all start letting them handle the burden of lessening the swag gap.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. She is a 2024 Forbes 30 Under 30: Media honoree, and her debut romance novella PASSING GAME is set to release March of 2027 (831 Stories/Simon & Schuster).

#6781. Sade Is No Ordinary Style Icon

An ode to the singer who was ahead of her time.

Has there ever been anyone as effortlessly ahead of her time as Sade? Born Helen Folasade Adu on Jan. 16, 1959, in Ibadan, Nigeria, the frontwoman of her namesake band has a core aesthetic that revolves around blindingly bright hoop earrings, a slicked-back bun, a deep red lip, and strong yet minimal silhouettes. As the band is inducted into the Rock & Roll Hall of Fame this weekend, there’s never been a better time to reflect on the British-Nigerian singer’s personal style. Think: sweaters with strong shoulders, thick belts on long cinched skirts, and, when called for, a perfectly eccentric evening jacket souped up with embroidery and satisfyingly present buttons.

Chalk it up to her era; Sade exploded in popularity in the ’80s, when these kinds of powerful silhouettes reigned supreme, but even beyond that, she knew the power of style. And it’s no wonder: Years ago, in a rare radio interview, she divulged that fashion was her passion long before music. “I made clothes. For three years I went to an art college and did fashion at Central Saint Martins in London,” she said. “I actually sold clothes, and that’s when I started to sing. I had to make a choice between doing the two, because I didn’t have enough time.” Though her foray into design was incredibly sparsely documented, her focus was on menswear, and she later co-founded the tailored menswear fashion label Demob with designer Sarah Lee, where the duo created club-kid-ready attire.

Onstage, whether she was singing “Smooth Operator” or “No Ordinary Love,” she wore her finishing touches with pride: lush little leather gloves in olive green to match a soft-shouldered woolen suit, or a festooning delicate statement necklace layered over a black tank top. Rather than layer multiple pieces, she communicates through the subtle impact of one-and-done statements. A delicate stack of bracelets, or modernist front-facing Lucite hoop earrings, for instance. She usually leans into white and black, except for the rare occasion when she chooses a jammy, rich shade of plum or deep olive green. Her attention to detail does not go unnoticed; there’s a clear knack for tailored silhouettes.

Sade’s elusive nature combined with the ethereal sound of her music also contributes to fashion forming her memorable identity. Long before others made a plain turtleneck or slicked-back hairstyle their uniform, there was Sade doing it authentically, and with accessorized flair. And in today’s era of highly produced musicians who are styled by a team, Sade still stands out.

Though Sade and her band will not be present at the ceremony, they called it “both a gift and a timely reminder to give thanks, not only to those who voted for us but also every single person who lets our music into their lives” in a statement. For every person who’s ever loved an accessory and a bold shoulder look (especially gold hoop earrings), Sade’s presence lives on beyond the music too.

#6775. All About Madelaine Petsch and Tyga’s Mysterious 2-Year Relationship Amid Those Breakup Rumors

#6774. Designers Say "Cook's Tables" Are About to Replace Kitchen Islands in 2027

Their versatility is just part of their appeal.

Pictured Above: A kitchen designed by Amy Sklar.

For years, oversized kitchen islands have been the be-all and end-all, but lately, designers and homeowners are gravitating toward something a bit smaller: a cook’s table.

Unlike built-in islands that can sometimes dominate a kitchen and cover most of the available floor space, cook’s tables bring a lot of flexibility to the room. Because they’re freestanding, you can move them around depending on your needs (especially if they’re on wheels), whether that’s for prepping meals, hanging out with your morning coffee, or helping kids with homework after school. They also tend to be narrower, making them the perfect solution for small kitchens.

The other benefit is the big charm factor they add. They’re often wooden, with traditional-looking design elements, like turned legs and curvy silhouettes. Cooks tables can also frequently be found in vintage and antique stores, so they’re a great way to work a bit of charm and patina into an otherwise utilitarian space.

As kitchens move away from ultra-sleek and minimal, pieces with warmth and character are becoming more and more popular, and a cook’s table is the perfect example of that. If you’re considering adding one to your own home, learn what to look for—and how to style it—below.

One of the biggest reasons cook’s tables are trending right now is that they soften the overall look and feel of a kitchen. “A traditional island can dominate a kitchen,” says designer Amy Sklar. Because cook’s tables don’t have a bulky built-in base, they feel visually lighter and less imposing, especially in kitchens where space is limited.

Designers also point to the emotional shift happening in homes post-pandemic. “The pandemic reshaped the way we live at home, placing greater value on spaces designed for connection and togetherness,” says designer Becky Ducsik of The Phinery. “Nowhere is this more apparent than in the kitchen, where homeowners are reimagining how they gather and entertain.”

Unlike traditional islands that are packed with appliances and storage, cook’s tables create a more communal focal point centered around gathering and spending time together. That relaxed atmosphere is part of what makes them feel so appealing. Designer Cynthia Lananh notes that cook’s tables strike a balance between practicality and personality, functioning as prep space, dining space, and entertaining hub all at once. “I love a kitchen island that looks like a piece of furniture,” she says, adding that the look feels more casual, approachable, and timeless than a standard built-in island.

While cook’s tables can absolutely make a statement in a large kitchen, designers say they’re especially effective in kitchens with tighter footprints or galley-style layouts. Because they take up less visual and physical space than a traditional island, they can help smaller kitchens feel more open and airy.

That said, larger kitchens can benefit from them too, just in a different way. “With the right vintage find or custom-crafted piece, it can anchor the room and become a striking centerpiece that adds both function and timeless appeal,” says Ducsik.

She recommends using cook’s tables as the focal point in kitchens where storage and appliances can be concentrated along the perimeter. Since seating typically wraps around all sides, she suggests leaving at least four to five feet of clearance around the table to maintain a comfortable flow.

For homeowners who want the look but don’t have space for a freestanding table in the center of the room, Ducsik also suggests integrating one as a peninsula extension at the end of cabinetry for a similar communal feel.

Unlike built-in islands, cook’s tables offer a level of flexibility that’s hard to beat. Since they’re freestanding, they can be moved or adapted more easily over time—a feature designers say is especially useful for entertaining.

Sklar points out that cook’s tables can create a flexible landing zone within a kitchen and are ideal for holidays or large gatherings when extra room is needed. “This is perfect for holidays and large gatherings where you need even more space,” Lananh says. She recommends adding casters if the table will double as both a prep and dining space, making it easier to move around as needed.

Scale is one of the most important things to get right: The table should feel proportional to the room while still leaving enough space to comfortably move around it. Height matters, too. Sklar notes that many vintage tables sit lower than standard counter height, so homeowners may need to modify them slightly with casters or leg extenders to make them more functional for prep work.

As for materials, wood remains the go-to choice for warmth and character. “Durable woods (like oak, maple, or walnut) are popular for their warmth, strength, and character,” says Lananh. “Reclaimed wood adds rustic charm, whereas metal or butcher block tops offer a more durable, industrial aesthetic.”

Ducsik echoes those sentiments and suggests echoing the table’s wood tones throughout the kitchen with details like open shelving, cutting boards, or serving pieces to create a cohesive look.

Lastly, focus on shape. Softer corners and curves can make the space feel more intimate, while rectangular tables can maximize seating and prep space. Ultimately, the goal is for the piece to feel like furniture, not cabinetry. And that’s exactly what makes cook’s tables so appealing in the first place.
