#6766. This 1904 Georgian Colonial Has Dramatic Period Details in Every Room—and a Titanic Connection

Designer Andrew Suvalsky balanced turn-of-the-century glamour with modern family life.

In Glen Ridge, New Jersey, a red-brick Georgian Colonial has a remarkable backstory. Known locally as the “Titanic House,” it was designed, built, and first owned in 1904 by Henry Blank, who later sailed aboard the Titanic—and survived. When a family of three bought the sprawling home in 2021, its history and architectural pedigree were part of the appeal, and they wanted to honor both while making the house feel like their own.

For designer Andrew Suvalsky, that meant striking the right aesthetic balance. “The biggest challenge was finding the sweet spot of grandeur and formality that is inherent to the house and its history, while making design choices that were suited to a more casual lifestyle,” he says.

Over a three-year renovation period, Suvalsky kept priceless details—like the original crown molding, gold architectural details, and the home’s many chandeliers—while giving the rest of the space a much-needed decor upgrade. “I’ve done traditional interiors, but this house is the most elaborate I’ve worked on, and the design had to meet the house on its level,” he notes.

The renovation touched nearly every room, from the billiards lounge to a fully remade kitchen. He also layered in luxe touches throughout: In the dining room, upholstered Fortuny panels bring a timeless elegance, while the bar’s walnut paneling and ornate cornucopia garland frame the fireplace.

As Suvalsky spent more time in the house, its grand scale began to feel more intimate. “It helped me understand how building a house this size made sense for a family 120 years ago and how it can translate to a family’s life today,” the designer says. He sees the resulting space as meaningfully in dialogue with its deep history while looking ahead to the family’s future. “It’s nice to know that good architecture can truly stand the test of time.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Andrew SuvalskyGeneral Contractor: Les Panek of LES Home ImprovementsStyling: Martin BourneLocation: Glen Ridge, New JerseyThe Space: A seven-bedroom, five-and-a-half-bathroom home across 12,000 square feet.

This room was designed as a proper sitting area that highlights the view—both of the landscape outside and into the surrounding foyer and bar. The sumptuous jewel tones throughout make for total eye candy, softening the formality of the space. “The plum grasscloth wall covering and dark Wedgwood blue trim are meant to feel dramatic, glamorous, and regal,” Suvalsky adds.

“The bar is reminiscent of the swankiest of lounges in a historic hotel, showcasing high-design elements from periods throughout the 20th century,” Suvalsky notes. Guests can sidle up to the centerpiece surface, comprised of dark walnut accented with blue opalescent tufted leather panels. Overhead, a Schumacher wallpaper provides the right amount of pattern play on the ceiling.

The Gilded Age grandeur continues into the billiards room, complete with an antique poker table and a pool table that had been in the home for generations. Suvalsky says the saturated baize hues were natural inspiration for the rest of the furnishings: “The deep blues and red and green touches play off the rug and give the feeling of an elaborate speakeasy.”

“Of all the new design elements, I’m most excited about the kitchen,” Suvalsky says. It had been about 25 years since its last renovation, and it was in need of a layout overhaul to increase flow and keep up with the owners’ love of cooking and entertaining.

With its white marble checkerboard floor and airier atmosphere, the room is an aesthetic departure from the home’s moody lounge areas, making it a more family-friendly gathering area. “The reference point here was a soft approach to a country English style kitchen that feels like it might have been built in the 1920s and refreshed with a midcentury palette and lighting,” Suvalsky notes.

One of the kitchen’s most notable design flourishes is the custom mint-green paint color Suvalsky chose not only for the cabinets and trim, but for the BlueStar range as well. “Clients increasingly want their range to be a major focal point, if not the focal point,” he notes. The cook space customizations didn’t stop there, though: He also relocated the warming drawer from its typical spot below the oven and installed it at mid-height as a regular drawer in the island, “to ensure it would get used daily,” he says.

As Suvalsky puts it, “This is the ultimate high-drama space.” The dining room contains many of the project's biggest investment pieces, such as the custom-designed, all-silk oval rug by Mark Nelson Designs, as well as the Fortuny upholstered wall panels. Bordering the space, gold and lacquered moldings have the look of a gilded confection.

With its French Provincial headboard and Venetian Fortuny ceiling fixture, the primary bedroom is transportive in both time and place. “Softened with European classical details, this bedroom is meant to feel calm and elevate daily life,” Suvalsky says.

Borrowing a similar palette and design sensibility as the primary bedroom, this adjoining dressing room contains “elements with a romantic feeling,” says Suvalsky.

Tucked under the grand main staircase, this powder room is “a beautiful little gem-like space,” according to Suvalsky. Despite its tiny footprint, this half-bath showcases a kaleidoscope of patterns, with geometric tiled floors, swirls of marble on the columnar sink, and sapphire-blue flora-and-fauna wallpaper.

Andrew Suvalsky is the founder of Andrew Suvalsky Designs, located in both New York City and Montclair, New Jersey. With his team of talented designers and architects, as well as the support from vendors and tradesmen, Suvalsky creates unique interiors that capture his clients' dreams without sacrificing functionality. Though the firm covers a range of design styles, clients can always count on Suvalsky to incorporate both antique and new pieces, as well as bespoke and custom furnishings.

#6765. Léa Seydoux Isn’t Trying to Act. She’s Trying to Live

The French actress continues her string of emotionally demanding roles in The Unknown and Gentle Monster

Léa Seydoux first stepped into the limelight in Christophe Honoré’s The Beautiful Person, where her role as Junie de Chartres earned her the Trophée Chopard award for rising actors at Cannes. From there, she moved seamlessly between highbrow French cinema and Hollywood, achieving a critical breakthrough with Blue Is the Warmest Color in 2013 and starring alongside Daniel Craig as a Bond girl in Spectre and No Time to Die. Most years, she’s starred in at least two films, and 2026 is no exception: She plays a woman with a man’s soul trapped in her body in Arthur Harari’s psychological fantasy The Unknown, and in Marie Kreutzer’s Gentle Monster, releasing on Netflix on November 18, she’s a pianist who unearths her husband’s hideous crimes. The words thorny and barbed read like understatements for both films, and it’s exactly what interests Seydoux about the stories. Like in life itself, she avoids easy answers, opting instead for exploration.

Seydoux got the chance to explore an especially ambitious idea in The Unknown. It tells the story of David Zimmerman (Niels Schneider), a photographer who wakes up in the body of a woman he sleeps with. Seydoux plays both that woman, Eva, and the man trapped within her body. “I felt the movie was something new to me, to play an impossible idea and make it believable, try to give it a truthful dimension,” Seydoux says. She’s no stranger to high-concept stories, previously starring in Bertrand Bonello’s The Beast and David Cronenberg’s Crimes of the Future. Each time, she imbues these characters with humanity, despite their far-fetched sci-fi dilemmas.

“I like movies that make you think, movies that bring up questions, when there’s a debate around the film,” she says. “What I love the most is to explore human nature, our human condition. This is what cinema allows us to do.”

Seydoux might vehemently love movies, yet acting remains a demanding process for her. She relies on the art form to dig into feelings and emotions. “I don’t feel necessarily very comfortable with acting. It’s not a place where I’m like, ‘I love it,’ but at the same time, I find it a bit painful. It’s more a way to experience things and ideas,” she says.

Gentle Monster provides another knotty premise, this time grounded in reality. It follows Lucy Weiss, a famed experimental pianist who gives up her career to raise her young son with her doting husband, Philip (Laurence Rupp). One day, the police knock on their door, and it’s revealed that Philip is accused of distributing child pornography.

“The subject matter is tough, but the fact that it’s through the woman’s perspective—and not through the victim or perpetrator—it’s through her emotions that the story unfolds [is what interested me],” she says. “Things are not too defined, but it’s also because of the subject matter; it was the only way to talk about it. [Marie] gave me a lot of freedom.”

Born in Paris in 1985, Seydoux spent her childhood wedged among seven siblings and half-siblings. She remembers feeling isolated, practically invisible, as a child. “I was always in the shadows in a way,” she says. “As a kid, I felt transparent. I was never at the center of [my] big family.” This plays a big part in her surprise at her acting career, never thinking of herself as someone deserving of center stage, of fame, of attention. In fact, acting never even crossed her mind until she befriended some actors when she turned 18. “I loved the way they lived their lives,” she says. “I admired their life—the fact that they were free.”

And yet, she rejects the label of “actress.” “I try to be truthful as much as possible,” she says. “I try not to act. This is what I try to be, and I’m more into reaction than action.” She adds, “It’s more reactive than acting.”

For Seydoux, “reacting” and really experiencing life is only possible if she “abandons” herself to her characters. One of the most intense examples of this is Abdellatif Kechiche’s controversial lesbian love story Blue Is the Warmest Color, in which she stars alongside Adèle Exarchopoulos. The film’s schedule stretched from two months to five months, and it has since become notorious for its grueling pace. (The famous sex scene took 10 days alone.) “The greatest and most intense experience I’ve had was with Adèle when we did Blue Is the Warmest Color,” she recalls. “It was crazy, and really the connection between us had to be very, very intense. I think, with Adèle, it’s something that will always be a part of me now. There was an exchange, and it was more than making a film. It was really a life experience.”

One scene from Gentle Monster lingered with me for weeks after I watched it: Lucy’s snotty, tearful confrontation with her husband at their family home as she pleads with him to tell the truth. This close-up of Seydoux not only accentuates her famous blue eyes but also shows her uncanny control of this character’s emotions. It feels, for lack of better words, real. Seydoux partly credits her empathy to being a mother of two children, but this scene goes beyond compassion; it shows how much of herself she gives to the character. Before filming this scene, Kreutzer offered little direction, letting her tap into how the character felt. “I prepare myself, and I have to just let myself go and be in the moment and just open up,” Seydoux says, suggesting that perhaps these emotions otherwise lie dormant within her.

I found this is what Seydoux means by “reaction.” Every role in her varied and extolled filmography functions as an avenue through which she can explore what it means to be human, what it means to feel. Her natural curiosity about the world and its inhabitants existed before she decided to act in her teens and will exist long into the future, even if acting jobs don’t remain on the table. “If it ended tomorrow, I’d be happy,” she says. “I will find a way. Now, I really feel that I’m acting because I still have the opportunity. But I have more things to explore, and I would love to have more experiences.”

#6763. The 6 Best Skin Care Tools for Women Over 50 That Are Worth the Investment, According to Experts

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Give your beauty routine an upgrade with these transformative tools.

There’s no shortage of skin care tools on the market. And if you’ve ever found yourself clicking through page after page of LED masks, microcurrent devices, and sculpting gadgets, wondering which ones are actually worth the investment, you’re not alone. With so many options to choose from, I set out to find the tools best suited for mature skin, focusing on the technologies that can complement a good routine rather than overwhelm it.

After 50, skin naturally begins to change. Collagen production slows, cellular turnover decreases, and hormonal shifts can bring new concerns like dryness, sensitivity, and changes in elasticity and pigmentation. But that doesn’t mean your routine needs to get more aggressive. In fact, the experts I spoke with emphasized quite the opposite: Mature skin benefits from thoughtful stimulation, consistency, and a healthy skin barrier—not from piling on increasingly intense treatments.

“Skin doesn’t need to be punished to improve,” says celebrity master esthetician Melissa Fox. Board-certified aesthetic physician associate at Row 89, Rocio Romero, agrees, pointing out that the best device is ultimately the one you’ll actually use consistently. After all, an expensive gadget collecting dust in your bathroom cabinet isn’t doing anything for your complexion.

After consulting experts, researching the options, and testing a few myself, I narrowed the crowded field down to six tools that stood out for their ability to support mature skin. Below, shop the best devices to improve firmness and radiance while temporarily reducing puffiness and sculpting the face.

“Red light is my number one,” says Dr. Romero. The wavelengths help regenerate cells and reduce inflammation. “There are different versions, and the trick is to pick the one that fits your lifestyle, because you have to be consistent with it,” she adds. This Celluma device is amazing because it has three modes (anti-aging, acne, and pain relief), and its large panel design works on your face and across your body.

Expert review: “Celluma is one of my favorite technologies because it’s gentle, noninvasive, and easy to incorporate into a consistent routine. Red and near-infrared light can help support collagen production and improve the appearance of fine lines over time. I especially love LED for mature skin because you’re encouraging healthier-looking skin without creating unnecessary inflammation. It’s a perfect example of progressive, not aggressive.”—Dr. Melissa Fox

Microcurrent devices like Nuface’s Trinity+ work wonders for lifting, tightening, and sculpting. Dr. Rocio recommends using the device on your face, neck, chest, and under-eyes. “Especially if you have Botox or filler, there are specific areas where it works beautifully,” she adds. “The contraction of the muscle will metabolize your injectables faster.”

Expert review: “Microcurrent is one of my favorite categories for women who want to address the appearance of facial contours. It uses a very low-level electrical current, and some people notice a temporary improvement in facial definition and tone. I particularly like it for clients focused on the jawline and cheek area.”—Dr. Melissa Fox

“Lyma is a great option for women who are serious about investing in advanced at-home skin care technology,” says Dr. Melissa Fox. It uses low-level laser light rather than the heat-based resurfacing approach associated with many professional lasers. It’s particularly helpful for those concerned about fine lines, texture, and overall skin quality.

Best for: Improving skin tone and elasticity

Customer review: “If you’re willing to put in the work, this will work for you! It is one of the few products that has actually changed my skin. It does take commitment, but it’s better than going somewhere for a pricey treatment. I just do it at home while watching TV!”

“Another tool that is so simple and you have at home already is ice,” says Dr. Romero. Dr. Fox concurs, saying, “Anyone who knows me knows I’m obsessed with cold therapy. I tell my clients to ice their skin daily, and cryo sticks are one of the easiest ways to incorporate cooling into a routine.” Cryo globes are the easy, luxe, and mess-free way to enjoy the process.

Expert review: “They’re wonderful for temporarily reducing puffiness, soothing the skin, and making the face look refreshed and more sculpted. I especially love them in the morning around the jawline, cheeks, and under-eye area. They don’t replace collagen-building treatments, but the immediate cosmetic benefits are beautiful.”—Dr. Melissa Fox

“Gua sha brushes are such an underrated tool,” says Dr. Fox. “Gua sha is my go-to,” Dr. Romero agrees. Gentle, deliberate movements can help temporarily reduce facial puffiness and relax areas where you hold tension, particularly around the jaw and brow.

Expert review: “As we age, the face can look more tired simply because of fluid retention or muscle tension. Gua sha is a wonderful way to bring movement and relaxation into a routine. It’s also a beautiful self-care ritual, although it won’t permanently lift the skin or replace collagen.”—Dr. Melissa Fox

With 17 at-home facial treatments and app-guided routines, the Ziip Halo uses microcurrent and nanocurrent technology to firm, lift, tone, sculpt, and smooth skin. “Think of it as nurturing the architecture of the face while celebrating the beautiful years that have shaped it,” says Kristen May Lee, an ITEC-Certified Esthetician. “Because the goal isn't to preserve the face we had at thirty, but to understand, nurture, and celebrate the skin we're living in now.”

Expert review: “As women enter their fifties, declining estrogen accelerates collagen loss, diminishes elasticity, and challenges the skin's ability to retain hydration. The Ziip Halo brings skincare beyond the topical, using a combination of microcurrent and nanocurrent electrical stimulation to support facial toning and the appearance of healthier, firmer, more radiant skin.”—Kristen May Lee

Melissa Fox, celebrity master esthetician and the founder of Flawless by Melissa Fox in Miami and West Palm Beach.

Frequency depends entirely on the technology. LED can often be used several times per week, or even daily when the device’s protocol allows it. Cooling tools can be incorporated daily, and gua shas can be used a few times a week with very gentle pressure. Microcurrent should follow the specific device’s instructions, though most recommend a few times per week as well. “My biggest advice is not to use everything at once,” says Dr. Fox. “More isn’t necessarily better, especially when skin becomes more reactive with age.” Choose two or three tools that address your concerns, stay consistent, and prioritize barrier health. Skin longevity is a marathon, not a sprint.

For this piece, ELLE associate beauty commerce writer Emma Aerin Becker consulted board-certified dermatologists to learn all about the best skin care tools for women over 50. She also tested them out and combed through hundreds of customer reviews, assessing each one based on its features and customer evaluations.

They’re barely noticeable but hugely helpful when cups runneth over.

Closet square footage, number of carats, the amount of protein you ate today—sometimes size does matter. That’s certainly true when it comes to a bra. #6761. Designers Say These 9 Unexpected Bed Styling Tricks Can Wake Up Your Whole Bedroom

These pillow ideas make your bed feel intentional, collected, and joyful.

A standard pillow setup—two king pillows against the headboard, with two standard pillows in the front, and a square accent pillow in the center—is a snooze. Designers are treating the bed as an opportunity to showcase a bit of flair. Not to mention awaken the entire bedroom.

What kinds of changes are happening? Throw and accent pillows are now treated as if they are fashion accessories. We’re seeing everything from edgy studs to romantic ruffles in contrasting colors to graphic lines that hypnotize. In fact, mismatched pieces work great and can feel “intentional, collected, and completely joyful,” says designer Betsy Wentz.

Here, nine unconventional ways to play with your bed pillow arrangements.

If the rest of your bedroom is neutral, designers are saying it’s OK to go more bold with your bed pillows. In the above space, it’s impossible not to notice the hypnotizing hue of Yves Klein blue pillows on a striped duvet cover.

“There’s something about that particular blue,” says designer Kate Anlyan, founder of Yasu Home. “It’s so vivid and almost electric, but it also has a depth to it. I love how it cuts through all the other tones without feeling disconnected from the rest of the room.” The hue works as a counterpoint to the wood paneling in the background and provides a balance to the visual weight of the black headboard.

The interconnection between the contrasting stripes on the square pillows and the round velvet ball feels both fun and sophisticated, says designer Beth Diana Smith. The stripes feel especially striking, bringing a sense of energy and definition to the space, when set against the moody purple-brown Benjamin Moore's Cinnamon Slate wall color. To keep the rest of the bedding from competing, Smith tames the room with a muted light gray coverlet.

“Match up the headboard, throws, lamps and artwork with your throw pillows to balance out color throughout your room,” says designer Renée Gaddis. The encapsulated look creates instant cohesion. Starting with a bold hue, like a mustard yellow, will make your job easier because it has personality from the start.

Faux fur is a classic way to add rich texture, but you don’t need to reserve it for a château. Interior designer Mialani Collard uses a neutral linen palette and tactile embellishments that sandwich the fluff to make it less prominent, allowing the bed to work even in tropical climates like Florida.

When you want a home with casual living as the main objective, fill surfaces in a way that doesn't look too pristine or orderly. Interior designer Tyson Ness of Studio Ness mixed different patterns and colors for the bedroom pillows so there’s no “right way” to make the bed after it’s been used.

“The different combinations create a slightly different look each time, keeping the overall feeling light, airy, and totally casual,” Ness adds.

Choosing pillows that match the bedding is easy, but Cate Gutter, owner and principal designer of CWG Design, prefers to add a bit of je ne sais quoi when she styles the bed.

“This bedroom is tucked away in a mountain home here in North Carolina, and we wanted it to have a playful, nostalgic feel reminiscent of summer camp,” Gutter says. “We carried that inspiration into the pillow designs, giving them an almost handmade, craft-like quality that feels whimsical.”

The oversized pillows are made in Imogen Heath's Arty Check, a cheerful yellow check finished with a fuzzy green fringe. In front of it, a smaller bolster is a playful take on a traditional shape, but the muted, multicolored stripe with a contrasting ruffled flange bolsters extra personality. The added texture breaks up the monotony of solid linens.

“Studs are an unexpected detail on bed pillows because they’re so versatile,” says Sean Carter, senior interior designer at Sapphire Pear. “They look just as great on a neutral fabric as they do with bold colors, prints, and patterns.” The metallic element adds a little edge without overpowering the overall design.

Wentz is no stranger when it comes to combining patterns and layering different scales in the same color family. Look for prints with their own personality that won’t compete with each other.

“The smaller confetti and stick stripe pillows give your eye a place to rest against that big graphic headboard, so they feel like friends, not strangers,” Wentz says.

Pillows gone wild can still balance restraint. Creating serious contrast—in pattern direction—will step up your bedscaping game.

“The geometric patterns in the pillows complement the strong linear elements,” says interior designer Nicole Johnson. “The vertical wood slats draw the eye upward, while the horizontal lines of the bed and furnishings provide balance.”

Linear patterns in the pillows at a smaller scale create a picture-in-picture effect that won’t overwhelm the overall design.

#6757. When Will the Next James Bond Movie Be Out? What to Know Following the Robert Pattinson Announcement

Now we know who 007 is, when can we expect to see him in action?

Robert Pattinson has officially been announced as the new 007, and now we’re left wondering: Just when will we see him on our screens as the famous secret agent in movie history?

The 40-year-old actor was confirmed earlier this afternoon to be playing the role of James Bond by Amazon MGM Studios. He said of taking on the famous spy role, “James Bond is one of the most legendary characters in cinema, and to be entrusted with 007 is the honor of a lifetime. I’m deeply grateful to Denis, Amy, David, Tanya, and everyone at Amazon MGM Studios for their belief in me. To say this role is a dream come true is an understatement. I couldn’t be more excited about this partnership and the work ahead with this fantastic team.”

Rob takes on the role following Daniel Craig’s departure as the spy in the most recent Bond movie No Time to Die in 2021.

Given it’s already been five years since we last had a James Bond movie, just how long will we have to wait for the next one? As of now, we don’t have an official release date for the new James Bond movie. However, given it’s November 2026 and Rob’s role has only just been announced, it seems unlikely we can expect the movie to be released next year.

According to an April 2026 report from The Sun, it’s unlikely that a new movie would be coming out until at least 2028, due to director Denis Villeneuve wanting to take a break after the Dune franchise comes to an end. Dune: Part Three, which also stars Rob, comes out on December 18, 2026.

A source told the publication, “Bond won’t be back until 2028. The production mindset’s gone from ‘Let’s hurry this through’ to ‘Make sure we get this right.’ Denis wanted a long break after Dune 3 and has some broad ideas on the script. Steven will get on with drafts, before they map out the final version. That’ll take at least the whole summer.”

They added, “Realistically, if they can make January 2027 work for pre-production, then that will be a success.”

As for when we can expect the movie to come out in 2028, Digital Spy reports there are two dates booked out in Amazon’s release calendar—April 21 and August 4—for an “Untitled Amazon MGM Event Film.”

In 2022, Amazon acquired MGM Studios, and in 2025, they reached an agreement for a new joint venture with Barbara Broccoli and Michael G. Wilson, who controlled the creative rights to Bond. This new venture meant that “Amazon MGM Studios will gain creative control of the James Bond franchise following closing of the transaction.”

And since February 2025, the hunt has been on for the next leading actor to take on the role of James Bond. Also reportedly said to be in the running for the role of Bond were Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, and Josh O’Connor, among (many) others.

#6756. This Unexpected French Antique Is Suddenly Making a Comeback, and You’ve Probably Seen It Before

The desire to preserve mementos from a wedding day transcends centuries and cultures. Today's brides still hang onto their wedding dresses, dry their bouquets, and spend a large portion of their budgets on photography. But in 19th- and 20th-century France, the art of preserving wedding-day memories took the form of globes de mariée, also known as marriage globes.

These ornate, shrine-like displays primarily showcased the bride's head crown and other keepsakes from the big day, and the additional pieces inside symbolized the couple's dreams for their life together. The cloche protected these special items from dust back then and still today.

Here's a closer look at the history and allure of globes de mariée, plus tips for sourcing and styling these gorgeous antiques.

Chris Featherston is the senior vice president of product acquisition at Austin Auction Gallery, and he and his family have been dealing in French antiques for more than 30 years. In the last couple of years alone, they've sold more than 150 globes de Mariée for an average of $400 to $600 each.

But this definitely wasn't always the case. Featherstone notes that the demand for these antiques has risen significantly in the past five years alone.

"In the early days of our business, most of what we sold came from house clearances in France," he recalls. "These globes were ubiquitous. At that time, we felt lucky to be able to sell them for $30 to $40 each."

Anyone with a strong appreciation for historic relics can see the appeal of these antiques. For instance, Mick and Paige Whitcomb have been collecting globes de mariée for nearly a decade, and they currently own 15. Their interest in these types of treasures is reflected in their careers. Mick creates sculptural lighting from architectural and industrial salvage dating back to the 1800s through his business, Artifacts by Nomad. Paige is a tintype photographer who captures staged scenes featuring antiques from the 1800s (including her globes de mariée) using a 19th-century large-format studio camera.

"While traveling in France for a wedding anniversary, we were first exposed to globes de mariée at the Marché aux Puces antique market in Paris," Mick says. "While we were initially caught by the beauty of the objects, after hearing their history and connection to French marriages, it felt like a beautiful way of commemorating our anniversary and trip to Europe."

Due to the highly custom nature of globes de marriée, each one is a unique find. However, Featherston notes that most contain a few similar components. For starters, the bride's headpiece often rests on a red cushion, and it typically shows off delicate beeswax orange blossoms that symbolize purity and virginity.

Most also contain small mirrors, which carried different meanings depending on the shape. For example, diamond-shaped mirrors symbolize how many children the couple wished to have, and rectangular mirrors stood for the length of time the couple knew each other before their engagement.

You'll also often find a wreath embellished with brass ornaments, usually flowers, birds and tree leaves. These symbolize love, a long-lasting marriage, and happiness.

"The globes de marriées integrate beautiful symbolism in every piece, and I find them fascinating," Paige says. "Each globe is so distinctive, and tells the story of the couple that created it. You can surmise the couple's hopes and dreams by minutiae tucked away behind the dome for safekeeping. I find very intimate details incorporated into the displays, such as a prayer card of a favorite saint, to photos of the bride and groom. I have even found hair and teeth, transforming the antique into a memento mori as well."

Mick has noticed that sourcing these antiques is considerably more difficult in the United States than in Europe. But with a little patience and determination, it is possible.

"Because of the glass domes, I think fewer importers are brave enough to bring them over," he says. "Despite this, we have found the occasional gem in the wild." In the States, he's had the most luck sourcing them from design-forward antique dealers, online auction sites and even prop houses that have just wrapped up a period film project.

Since there is a constant demand for these pieces, Featherston tries to feature several in every sale at Austin Auction Gallery.

Globes de mariée can add a formal and traditional flair to any space, and they even mix well with more modern accents.

"One of my favorite ways to showcase the globes de mariée is to mix them with a piece of contemporary art," Paige says. "I love to create a harmony between our collections of antiques and artwork from artists that are living and working today."

For example, Mick and Paige have styled two of their favorite globes de mariée with the piece "The Gardener" by working artist L.J. Eidolon. "I find collecting modern works that have a historic feeling to them helps create balance between the multiple centuries displayed together in one scene," Paige says.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6754. Elias Matso Is the Twisted Corsetier Dressing Sydney Sweeney and Jenna Ortega

The young designer talked to Bazaar about craftsmanship, fittings, and developing his signature technique

These days, Elias Matso is known for twisted corsetry worn by the likes of Sydney Sweeney and Jenna Ortega. But the first time he noticed the impact of a good costume, he was a kid watching Julie Andrews in Victor/Victoria. He sat by the TV watching the Oscar-winning actor perform “Le Jazz Hot!” in Art Deco gowns, decadent headpieces, and dramatic feathered capes. “I know she was acting in that scene, but I just felt like she was so happy to be wearing those costumes,” he tells Harper’s Bazaar. “And I was obsessed with that.”

Matso is now 24, and it’s easy to see the impact of that moment on his creative approach. A recent graduate of Polimoda in Florence with Charles James, Bob Mackie, Mugler, and Mr. Pearl on his mood board, he crafts avant-garde, couture corsetry. (It’s perhaps no surprise that he has a history of making costumes for drag performers.) His approach is personal and intimate, rooted in hand craftsmanship and collaborative dialogue with clients.

In October 2025, Sweeney made waves when she wore a crystal naked dress, pulled from Christian Cowan’s Spring/Summer 2026 collection and made in collaboration with the young designer. The piece featured twisted corsetry and a semi-sheer body that hugged the Euphoria star’s body—and, naturally, the public had quite a lot to say about it. “I did not anticipate that it would be as big as it was,” the artist admits. “It was really cool that the conversation left fashion and became a bigger conversation about women’s bodies and what modesty should look like or shouldn’t look like. And how a lot of the world doesn’t know what boobs look like.”

While the discourse surprised Matso, it reflected one of his own core beliefs: that “fashion is at least 50% the person who’s wearing the thing.” He keeps this in mind during fittings, ensuring clients feel comfortable and beautiful every step of the way. Below, he talks to Bazaar about his early references, his focus on craftsmanship, and the colorful steel-boning dress recently worn by Jenna Ortega.

As a kid, I had a paper doll of myself, and whenever there were clothes that I wanted that I didn’t have, I would make them for the paper doll. It was pocket sized, and whenever I was bored—which was all the time, because I have attention deficit issues—I would pull it out and play.

It is. It was my third year and I wanted to explore the tension that children hold, when they feel like they’re growing faster than they can keep up with, and feeling at odds with themselves, because that’s how I felt when I was a kid. There was this feeling, especially in the queer world, of being pulled in different directions, gender-wise. And so I had developed that idea, and then through sketching and conversations with my teachers, we started getting more and more towards this very literal idea of a twist. And then the collaboration that I did with Christian Cowan pushed it further.

I was in New York over the summer before my senior year, and I was looking for jobs and not getting anything. I started looking into scooping ice cream at Van Leeuwen’s and stuff like that. And then, one day, I got a text from Alicia Keys’s stylist and a DM from Christian that was like, “Do you want to do some dresses for my show?” It was a good day.

It actually wasn’t originally supposed to be that fabric. We couldn’t find the fabric we wanted, and we couldn’t figure out how to put as many stones as we needed onto the fabric that we found. Then we just found this sheer fabric, and we were like, “Okay, it’s going to be the original dress, but it’s going to be completely see-through.”

I made it to fit this gorgeous six-foot-tall model from Finland. And this is why fit is so fun, because with that dress, there was this miracle where it fit her so perfectly one way, and then it fit Sydney, who has completely different proportions, in this totally other, also beautiful way. Often when things get pulled from a runway, they don’t fit like that. And it was just really cool to see.

I really like meeting people in person when I can because it just always creates a better outcome. I feel like the conversation is so much about your personality, but also your physiology, the way you’re built and the way you’re made up. People’s bodies respond differently. So it’s really important to do fittings and to check in with people before, during, and after the fitting, and ask, “How intense of an experience do you want this to be? What will make you feel the most beautiful, the most comfortable?”

Sometimes a little bit of discomfort is part of being comfortable, but you have to have signed up for that. That’s my thing that I’ve learned: Discomfort, if you’re prepared, can be part of it. But there has to be a conversation if discomfort is involved, or else we are forgetting that fashion is at least 50% the person who’s wearing the thing.

When I start a collection, I’m always looking through the archives of a museum, like the Whitney or often the Guggenheim, because you have less control over what you’re going to find. [For this piece,] I had found one painting that led me to Mark Ranger’s website. And we got in touch and then collaborated the whole year. It turns out we explore a lot of the same themes. Here, the dress was supposed to be about time and our body’s relationship to gravity and this idea of melting, not in a way that we’re trying to resist and slow down, but in a way that’s just natural and beautiful.

The goal was to make something unrecognizable, but using recognizable techniques. I already had a lot of steel boning because it’s the same stuff that’s inside all these corsets. And so I just got more, and then I started chopping it up into smaller and smaller pieces. I was able to create a relationship with Preciosa, who does crystals for a lot of big brands—I met them through a trip that they did to my school. I just came up with this weird technique that was, I guess, a test of my own physical and mental strength in a way.

I didn’t make it with Jenna in mind. I never thought it would have all these different lives and stages.

I guess I’m seeing it less and less valued in fashion. And even when something is hand-done and comes out gorgeous and successful, the people that wind up getting the praise for those things are not the people who do the thing, but the people who have curated those artisans—the artistic directors, the designers. My friends and I have just graduated, we’re looking for work, and I see my friends who have really singular skills getting so undervalued for their work and their time. That is something that is really frustrating to me. And in an age where so many things can be falsified via technology and the internet, I think things that you can’t fake are how something’s made, how something stands up over time, how something feels on the inside when you put it on. And designers care less and less about that, I guess.

I’m in a transitional stage where I’m not sure what I’m gonna do next. Every day, I get people asking me to make dresses on this scale for free or for bafflingly low amounts of money. You know, I’m told all the time that there’s no budget for things. People want couture for no budget. People in this industry have no shame when it comes to asking for that. For emerging designers, there are so many of us that they know that if I can’t supply what they’re looking for, they will find another independent designer who has the resources or the funds or the ability to provide that thing for less than what it’s worth. I do worry about that. But this press is making me think that maybe there is a niche for something that walks the line between art and fashion and not in the aesthetic sense, which people talk about all the time, but in the financial and logistical sense. So that’s what I’m trying to figure out.

Also, I found that if people are asking for things for free, it’s very rarely the client themselves. It’s often their team. I love the art of styling, but that is another reason that I find one-on-one relationships and conversations with the person who you’re going to be dressing really important. It makes them understand the level of attention or care that they are interacting with.

This is still in process, but I’m working on a piece with Laverne Cox. I always wanted to work with her. And then starting to work with her, I was like, “I was right.” Because she has such appreciation and respect for the process of everything and the details and how everything works. She’s a really respectful and engaging patron, and we’ve had a lot of interesting conversations about the designers that we both look up to.

For me, I want to take the momentum that I have and do something that feels exciting with it. And at the same time, I am not sure that I have the experience to start a company, you know? I am in conversations with lots of people who I’ve always wanted to collaborate with, and it’s so exciting. Really, really exciting and mind-blowing. And at the same time, I’m reaching out to brands that I admire to see what openings they have because we all need stability. I’m trying to remain as flexible as possible.

#6752. Robert Pattinson Is Our New James Bond—And Seemingly the Only Leading Man Left in Hollywood

He’s Batman, he’s Bond, and he’s seemingly in every other film these days. But has Hollywood’s obsession with Robert Pattinson exposed a bigger problem?

Having already dominated our cinema screens in 2026 with The Drama, The Odyssey, and Primetime, and with Dune: Part Three still to come, Robert Pattinson has somehow found room in his diary to take on another rather significant commitment: James Bond.

Yes, the man currently playing Batman is also our new 007. Somewhere, a casting director’s Rolodex is gathering dust.

At this point, I’m half expecting the British actor to pop up in a director’s cut of The Devil Wears Prada 2 wearing a cerulean jumper. Then again, he did once profess his love of taking jobs from younger actors. Clearly, he wasn’t joking.

Now, don’t get me wrong, I’m hardly immune to Rob’s charms. As someone who lived through the cultural juggernaut that was Twilight, watching his transformation from sparkly vampire to one of the most charismatic actors of his generation has been a sight to behold. But his latest appointment begs the question: Is there only one leading man left in Hollywood?

For months leading up to the announcement, reports suggested the race to become Bond had been narrowed down to Damson Idris and Aaron Taylor-Johnson. Then Callum Turner’s name entered the mix, alongside Jack Lowden, Jacob Elordi, and Paul Mescal, followed by Nicholas Hoult, Josh O’Connor, and, at the eleventh hour, Robert himself. At one point, I was half-tempted to put myself forward for the role.

Yet, in hindsight, Rob’s casting perhaps shouldn’t come as much of a surprise. Bond will mark his second collaboration with director Denis Villeneuve before their first, Dune: Part Three, has even reached theaters. Christopher Nolan has already cast him twice (in Tenet and The Odyssey) while Matt Reeves is reuniting with the actor for The Batman Part II.

For all the talk of Hollywood’s movie star drought, its most influential filmmakers seem to have found their guy. And they’re not particularly keen on letting him go. Commercially, you can understand the appeal. The Odyssey alone has amassed more than $1.8 billion at the global box office, making it the second-highest-grossing film of 2026 so far behind Spider-Man: Brand New Day.

Of course, those ticket sales aren’t solely down to Robert Pattinson. Christopher Nolan’s name and the incredible ensemble cast of Zendaya, Tom Holland, Matt Damon, and Anne Hathaway, among others, carry considerable weight, but Rob’s ability to move between intimate independent films like The Lighthouse and sprawling blockbusters makes him an increasingly valuable commodity.

According to Box Office Watch, the five films Rob has appeared in over the past two years have collectively grossed more than $2.1 billion worldwide. And that’s before he sets foot on Arrakis to face Paul Atreides and the Fremen in December.

However, what’s interesting about the British actor becoming Hollywood’s ultimate leading man is that he’s spent much of his career actively dismantling that very archetype.

Look at his recent filmography: a fiancé whose behavior in The Drama triggers an intervention of sorts; the rather unpleasant Antinous in The Odyssey; a morally complicated exploration of To Catch a Predator’s Chris Hansen in Primetime; and the hapless Jackson grappling to support his partner in the throes of postpartum depression in Die My Love. Even his Batman is more emotionally unavailable sad boy than conventional superhero.

Speaking to The Guardian in July, veteran film journalist Steven Gaydos described Robert as “an accomplished and fearless actor with tremendous range,” praising his refusal to simply give audiences what they expect.

Perhaps that’s precisely what makes him so appealing to filmmakers. But there’s a difference between recognizing an actor’s talent and an industry becoming overly reliant on it.

Historically, Bond has been a vehicle for turning actors into international superstars. Rob’s casting arguably reverses that relationship. For Amazon MGM Studios, entrusting its first Bond outing to an established star with a multigenerational fan base feels like a particularly safe bet.

But where does that leave the next generation of leading men? How do we discover another Robert Pattinson if Hollywood keeps casting Robert Pattinson?

Perhaps Hollywood doesn’t have a leading man problem so much as an imagination problem. And perhaps the next great movie star is already out there, waiting for someone to take the same chance on them.

After all, even Robert Pattinson had to be discovered once—no thanks to Jack Whitehall’s dad.

#6750. I Just Finished My Kitchen Renovation, and This “Hidden” Island Feature Is the One Upgrade I’d Recommend to Everyone

It’s made my cook space so much more functional.

I’m always noticing something cool in someone else’s home and thinking, “Oh, I need that, too.” That happened last year when my family stayed with my in-laws. It was my first time seeing their newly renovated kitchen, and after a quick tour, one small detail caught my eye: the pop-up outlet in the kitchen island.

It was simple, practical, and exactly the kind of feature I wanted in my own kitchen. When we recently renovated our cook space, it went to the top of our wish list—and it has indeed been a game-changer with how we use the area.

Ahead, here’s why I love having a pop-up outlet, what to consider before installing one, and a few other kitchen gadgets that you might want to consider adding to your next upgrade.

Like the name suggests, a pop-up outlet is basically an electrical outlet that hides inside your countertop until you need it. Most models have a top you press or pull, and the outlet rises up so you can plug in your blender, mixer, or whatever else you're using. When you're done, you push it back down and it's out of sight.

There are a few different versions, including ones that pop up manually and others that rise with the push of a button. Some, like the one we have, include USB ports or wireless charging built in. The outlet connects to power underneath the countertop, so it's something you'll want to plan for before your new counters go in.

Like pretty much any kitchen gadget, there are a range of options available from budget to high end. We opted for something mid-tier that still included the main functions we wanted like the additional USB ports and the charging capability on the top. If you’re a chronic “let your phone get down to one or two percent before finally charging it” person like me, I highly recommend this.

There also are some that will go nearly fully flush with the countertop, and can even have part of the countertop material on the top of it to look seamless. We did not go this route, but if you prefer the look of a flawless countertop, this might be the way to go.

We put ours in the corner of our kitchen where it’s almost entirely hidden by decor. That corner of the kitchen was otherwise without any spots to plug in appliances, so this fixed multiple problems by having electricity access while remaining discreet.

You can really put them anywhere you want, but keep in mind where you’ll need extra power accessibility and where it won’t be an eyesore, if that’s a concern. I’m also a big fan of in-drawer charging outlets.

There was some back-and-forth about adding this pop-up outlet to our kitchen remodel budget. The outlet itself isn’t break-the-bank expensive, but there were extra expenses that went with it, like hiring an electrician and paying to have a hole cut in the new countertop.

Though we decided it was worth the expense, here are some of the pros and cons you should consider if you’re on the fence.

I’m always so impressed by kitchens that integrate small, helpful gadgets like this one into their design. Here are a few other popular additions that we either made or were thinking about adding to our renovation.

Maybe it’s just a me thing, but I feel like the harder it is to find an appliance or trash can, the fancier a kitchen is. The pull-out trash can was integrated into our kitchen peninsula, and we purchased a more expensive panel-ready dishwasher so it looked like a regular cabinet. An integrated refrigerator was almost twice as expensive as a regular one, so we did not go that route.

With the way our kitchen is laid out, we unfortunately have two awkward corner cabinets. For one, we upgraded with blind corner cabinet hardware, which was well worth it.

We did not integrate a bottle rinser into our current kitchen design, but we did in our last two kitchens and loved them. It got so much use when our two kids were babies, since we could quickly rinse out bottles. They’re also great for wine drinkers or anyone who hosts a lot of parties.

Admittedly, this one is controversial, as some designers don’t like this feature. But personally, we love the look and it’s next on our “cool kitchen wish list.”

He has written for multiple home improvement magazines and websites, drawing on his own experience renovating homes with his wife to create practical, inspiring content for homeowners and DIY enthusiasts alike.

#6749. Emma Watson’s Rare Appearance Came With a Vintage Valentino Look

Emma Watson may be keeping a low profile these days, but she hasn’t lost her eye for a good vintage find.

The actress stepped out on Thursday night at GQ’s Men of the Year party in Los Angeles, where she joined guests including Ayo Edebiri, Monica Barbaro, Tinashe, and more at the Chateau Marmont.

For the occasion, Watson wore a vintage patterned piece from Valentino’s collaboration with artist Christi Belcourt for its 2016 resort collection. She paired the colorful design with black-and-white striped Jacquemus stilettos for a lesson in pattern mixing and pairing.

The actress skipped the red carpet arrivals but was photographed outside the venue, staying relatively under the radar despite the star-studded guest list.

The outing marks another occasional appearance from the Harry Potter alum, who has largely stepped away from acting since completing work on Greta Gerwig’s Little Women in 2019. Earlier this month, she attended another Los Angeles event, the LACMA Art+Film Gala, in a sculpted ruby gown.

In a 2025 interview with Hollywood Authentic, Watson spoke about the decision to take a break from the industry, explaining that while she misses the creative process, she doesn’t miss the demands of promoting films.

“I do not miss selling things,” she said, describing that promotional side as “quite soul-destroying,” before adding that she does “very much miss using my skill set, and I very much miss the art.”

Watson has since explored other creative pursuits, including directing and her work on spirits brand Renais, which she co-founded with her brother, Alex. While she hasn’t announced a return to the screen, she has made it clear that she remains open to the right project and intent on being around people who share a passion for film.

“While I might not be making work right now, I still do feel that I’m part of a community,” she said in 2025. “I want to stay connected to that community and be part of it.”

#6747. From Harry Styles to Audrey Hobert, These Major Artists Are Noticeably Missing From the 2027 Grammy Nominations

It’s that time of year again. Just as festival season comes to a close, awards season is already off to the races. While musicians and artists do it for the love of the game, you have to admit: getting a shiny trophy and industry-wide recognition from your peers is kinda nice.

While this year’s Grammy nominations included a ton of pleasant surprises (ahem, RAYE’s 11 nominations, Remi Wolf’s Best New Artist nod, and some posthumous recognition for the late Dolly Parton), they seemed to fall flat in a few areas. I’ll say it: Overall, I’m not that pressed over the 2027 nominations and am excited to see acts like Tinashe and underscores finally get recognition from the Recording Academy.

But known Grammy darlings like Harry Styles and Doja Cat were seriously snubbed this year, along with rising acts like Audrey Hobert and Malcolm Todd. So, without further ado, and in true Cosmo tradition, here are the biggest snubs from the 2027 Grammy nominations.

After seeing Audrey Hobert transform from behind-the-scenes collaborator with her (equally snubbed!) bestie, Gracie Abrams, into a front-line singer-songwriter, I thought she’d be a shoo-in for Best New Artist this year. I was an early adopter when I proclaimed “Sue Me” as the song of summer 2025, and her debut album as one of the best albums of the year. Since then, she’s been the shooting star pop music has been craving with diary-entry songwriting and intimate sleepover vibes on stage—though she’s performed to thousands at Lollapalooza and beyond.

Speaking of Audrey, her younger brother and fellow musician, Malcolm Todd, was also expected to potentially earn recognition in the Best New Artist category. Not only did he drop his third album Do That Again in June, but he also had several of his previous hits like “Earrings” and “Obsessica” take off on TikTok. Not to mention, Best New Artist nominee Don Toliver featured a sample of Malcolm’s track, “Chest Pain (I Love),” on his hit, “E85.” At least he landed that subtle nod!

My man Harry is a known Grammy darling. He’s swept in the past, but this time around, he’s been seriously snubbed. For his fourth album, Kiss All the Time. Disco, Occasionally to not be recognized at all—let alone in any of the major categories—feels strange. Some will argue that it “didn’t hit the same” as his other albums, but after taking some time off and finding his way back to the club, he dropped an incredible body of work in KISSCO. Our intro to the record is his five-minute lead single “Aperture,” which reminded us all that the revolution might take place on the dancefloor.

Sienna Spiro rightfully earned a nod in the Best New Artist category after winning the same award at this year’s VMAs. If she has a similar pipeline to Chappell Roan, she might just take home the golden gramophone, too. Her breakout moment comes off the success of her mega-viral ballad, “Die on This Hill.” And because ROTY honors production, the stunning string arrangements and her heart-wrenching vocal performance deserved some recognition here.

Another snub I’m shocked by is Noah Kahan. I personally don’t go here, but I know so many industry insiders and personal friends who ride hard for Noah, and I think they’re right to do so. His songwriting chops have made him one of the most successful artists of the streaming age, and to see his massive 2026 track “The Great Divide” get paid dust in the core categories feels out of character for the Recording Academy. (Though, his album of the same name earned a nod in the Best Contemporary Folk Album category.)

As much as I love Doja Cat, I’m not entirely surprised by her snub here. “Jealous Type,” as well as her Jack Antonoff-produced album, Vie, was a campy ode to the ’80s and should have been a major contender here. It’s unclear whether she submitted anything for consideration this year, but I find it hard to believe given Jack’s involvement and her track record as a Grammy darling. This era deserved more, but it’s not entirely a loss—I can see it having a bit of a resurgence in a few years.

Say it with me: JUSTICE FOR WEST END GIRL! Though Lily Allen and her art directors, Leith Clark and Nieves González, rightfully earned a nod in the Best Album Cover category, she didn’t land any in the core four categories. She made a powder-blue polka-dot puffer coat instantly recognizable, and along with a Duane Reade bag, it’ll serve as “gay Halloween” costume inspo for decades to come.

But back to the music—Lily hadn’t released an album since 2018 before she poured her heart out on West End Girl. It’s a deeply confessional record about her personal life and the downfall of her public marriage with Stranger Things star David Harbour, but it was also a comeback for the ages. To lay everything out as she did takes serious guts, and she did it the only way she knows: with her signature wit and deliciously unexpected production choices, standing ten toes down. (While…maybe wearing a dress printed with actual receipts and text messages, or a bib that reads “CUCK” in all caps.) Lily, I never doubted you!

#6744. 5 Ways Warmth Has Shaped Interior Design in 2026, According to Experts

Warmth is more than a palette—it’s the secret to a home you never want to leave.

After years of cool neutrals and minimalist interiors, there’s been a shift toward warmer, more inviting spaces that feel layered, personal, and lived-in.

“Warmth has become more than just a color palette or a particular material. It's about spaces that feel authentic, comfortable, and reflective of the people who live in them,” says Laura Gerber, founder of ODE Design.

At the end of the day, a warm home should feel inviting and effortless, with materials and details that make people want to spend time there. If you’re looking to add some warmth to your home, take inspiration from these designer-approved ideas.

Wood’s organic grain brings instant warmth to both modern and traditional homes, adding depth without making a space feel overly formal.

“We’re incorporating more white oak, walnut, and rift-cut cabinetry than we have in years, along with wood ceilings and paneled walls,” explains Rachel Simpson, senior interior designer at Revive Design and Renovation.

There’s been a shift from stark white countertops toward natural quartzite, travertine, and marble with warmer veining. These materials have natural variation and movement that bring character into a space.

“Honed finishes, in particular, feel softer and more organic than high-polish surfaces. They allow the beauty of the stone to speak for itself without feeling overly formal or glossy,” explains Simpson.

These days, people really want their homes to feel like a sanctuary.

“In a recent project, the client envisioned a grown-up treehouse nestled among the evergreens. We wanted to create a warm yet contemporary mountain retreat that felt luxurious but also comfortable and welcoming,” says Charles Greenfeld, founder and principal designer at Ethan Charles Design.

Rather than relying on one or two dramatic statement pieces, the interiors were designed to be experienced as a whole, with each detail contributing to that warm aesthetic.

Creating warmth is really about creating layers of thoughtfully curated design elements. This helps to create a home you want to enter and never leave.

“Well-proportioned rugs, textures on walls, loads of art, draperies, and lighting overhead and at eye level are just some of the things we can add to our spaces to make them feel cozy and accessible,” says Ashley Powell, cofounder and interior designer of Roseberry Allen.

Designers have lately been drawn to rooms where the colors are close to one another, but the materials do different things.

“A woven rug, a textured wall, and a piece of wood with a visible grain each catch the light differently,” says Jess Klein, founder and principal of Jess Klein Studio.

That variation adds visual depth and makes the room feel warmer and more inviting, even when the color palette is understated.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6742. Dakota Johnson and Machine Gun Kelly’s Full Relationship Timeline

Dakota Johnson and Machine Gun Kelly have yet to define their relationship publicly, but the two stars have been spending plenty of time together.

The actress and musician were first linked in September 2026, when they were photographed together in New York City. Days later, a source told People that Johnson and Kelly, whose real name is Colson Baker, “have been hanging out and seeing each other under the radar for months now.”

The two stars had known each other before, but according to the source, they became closer after attending Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce’s wedding in July.

Since then, Johnson has watched Kelly walk the runway in Milan, and sources have shared more about how their connection developed.

Below, everything we know about Johnson and Kelly’s rumored relationship so far.

Johnson and Kelly both attended Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce’s July 3 wedding at Madison Square Garden in New York City.

The two stars already knew each other, but a source close to Kelly told People in September that they “hit it off” at the wedding.

On September 12, Johnson and Kelly were photographed together in New York City, getting out of the same SUV and entering Swift’s Tribeca apartment.

The source explained that Johnson and Kelly “started off as friends and were texting often, but now they’re spending more time together.”

When asked whether she ever feels compelled to correct speculation about her personal life, Johnson explained why she prefers not to engage with it.

Two days later, on November 5, Johnson and Kelly stepped out for another evening together in Los Angeles, attending a Khaite dinner at Dan Tana’s in West Hollywood. The next week, Johnson and Kelly stepped out for another evening together in Los Angeles on November 11. The following day, the pair was spotted outside of the studio again. This time, Johnson wore a floor-length black two-piece set with a floral pattern.

#6741. How ‘Rivals’ Created an Age-Gap Relationship We’re Actually Rooting For

R ivals has returned to our screens, and Rutshire’s residents are hornier than ever. But Rutag fans might have to hang on a little longer to see them enter their relationship era. Frustrating? Not necessarily, as maybe this intense slow-burn is exactly the reason Rivals has managed to create an age-gap relationship we’re actually rooting for.

There is a lot of awareness around age-gap couples in pop culture right now, and audiences seem very done with the classic younger woman, older man dynamic. Movies like A24’s Babygirl and Amazon’s The Idea of You have gone some way toward challenging outdated tropes around age-related power dynamics, but that still doesn’t erase how Hollywood handled things in the past (Pretty Little Liars and Fifty Shades of Grey come to mind).

Rivals is a bit of an anomaly in how it has tackled Rupert and Taggie’s age-gap relationship so far, and the slower pace seems to have played a very necessary part in that. Rutag’s slow-burn underpins the entire foundation of their connection. What’s working for me personally is that Rupert hasn’t been rushing Taggie emotionally or physically to match where he’s at in his current stage of life. In fact, even though he seems besotted with her—she’s sort of become his moral touchstone—he’s doing his best to stay away from her. He seems to find the idea of letting her down (or fully letting her in) absolutely terrifying, despite how much he clearly cares.

Rivals gains further grace from viewers for its willingness to openly acknowledge Rupert and Taggie’s age gap as being an obstacle to overcome in their relationship, rather than just glossing over it or choosing to pretend it doesn’t exist. Rupert even acknowledges the age gap as being an issue himself by making a promise to Declan in season 1 that he’ll stay away from Taggie. By showing us these hurdles early on, it gives deeper insight into what a relationship would eventually mean for these two and what they’d be up against.

Watching Taggie gain life experience and have her own adventures of self-discovery—not everything has to involve Rupert—has helped them remain exciting to us as a potential couple. Her catering business seems to be thriving, for example. The slow-burn gives Taggie the opportunity to forge her own identity and ambitions, which would be important to have going into a relationship with Rupert, given their differences in life experience. Taggie is slowly blossoming into herself, without falling into that cliché trap of trying to change who she is to impress a man, and that’s empowering and necessary.

As long as Rivals continues to explore the age gap with care and consideration, I think fans will keep rooting for Rutag’s romance, no matter how long the slow-burn takes.

#6739. MobLand Season 2’s Most Striking Watches

From Pierce Brosnan’s Omega to Paddy Considine’s Breitling.

The Harrigan men don’t do flashy. For a family sitting on a drugs fortune that stretches from north London to a sprawling estate in the Cotswolds, they dress like they’re popping out for a pint: good tailoring, wax jackets, country staples, and nothing with a logo you could spot from across the room.

Their watches are no exception. There’s nothing loud or iced ou—just good, solid classics chosen by men who know their stuff. And they really do: In the very first episode, it turns out that one of the family’s side hustles is trading stolen cars for watches. So, somewhere in the Cotswolds, a Harrigan is almost certainly arguing about reference numbers on r/Watches.

Look closer, though, and the watches say more than just “good taste.” Each matches the job the man does for the family, so that watch tells you exactly where he sits in the pecking order. From the boss (Pierce Brosnan) down to the man who’s doing his dirty work, here’s how they line up.

Pierce Brosnan plays Conrad, the family’s head honcho—the guy who runs the empire without getting mud on his brogues (most of the time, anyway). And when you're at the top, you don’t need a watch that does much. No stopwatch, no dive bezel, no second time zone (that’s what the staff are for). So, Conrad wears a dress watch, and a decorative one at that: a gold Omega Constellation, with a fluted bezel and pie-pan dial that are there, purely, to look good. Like Conrad, though, it’s tougher than it looks: the Globemaster was Omega’s first Master Chronometer, tested to fend off magnetic fields of 15,000 gauss.

Then there’s the in-joke: Brosnan wore an Omega Seamaster during his time as 007, so seeing him return to his roots is a little Easter egg for anyone who’s paying attention. The sporty diver has been swapped with something more expensive but less practical—would you expect anything less for a retired Bond?

Kevin is the heir—the son, played by Paddy Considine, who’s waiting for that top job with one eye firmly on his dad’s will. So, fittingly, his watch feels a little eager. It’s a Breitling Premier B01 (the flash of yellow under the strap gives it away). A strong watch, for sure, it’s just the kind you buy when you want to look like the boss before you’ve secured the promotion. And it tracks: When Willy Breitling designed the OG Premier in the 1940s, it was the brand’s first watch made purely for style.

The real tell, though, is that it’s still working harder than his dad’s. Conrad’s gold Omega just tells the time, because Conrad hasn’t had much else to do in years. Kevin’s chronograph comes with pushers, sub-dials, and a stopwatch, plus around 70 hours of power reserve. The watch of a man who’s close to the throne, but not quite there yet.

Paul (Emmett J. Scanlan) is the Harrigans’ head of security and Conrad’s right-hand man—the one who’s always just a step away. And when the family’s estate comes under attack in Season 2, he, of course, is the one holding the fort. So, as you’d expect, there’s no gold here. Paul, instead, gets a tool watch.

But it’s not just any old tool watch, it’s a serious tool watch: IWC’s Pilot’s Chronograph. From a long line of sturdy watches built for military pilots who needed to tell the time in an instant, it has a large steel case, big clear numerals, and it’s tough enough to survive being bashed around a little (which, in Paul’s line of work, is a regular occurrence). This one’s a vintage mechaquartz version which is a bit of a hybrid: a quartz movement keeps the time on track while the chronograph is mechanical. Part brains, part brawn. Sounds like the perfect head of security to us.

#6738. Is Belrose University From ‘Campus Drivers’ Real? Let’s Break Down the Dreamy Filming Locations

Living vicariously through the characters in a romance show is always fun, but when the love story unfolds in a gorgeous location, it makes the fantasy even better. Are you enjoying the French series Campus Drivers on Prime Video, and feeling the itch to start exploring the area on Google Maps, Airbnb, and even Zillow? These Campus Drivers filming locations will get you started.

The series follows a college student named Lois, who becomes temporarily homeless after getting dumped by her boyfriend/roommate and moves in with her neighbor. He happens to have a ride-share app, which is definitely not just for transporting drunk coeds he and his friends want to hook up with, and is named Lane. Normal meet-cute stuff. Also, Lois and Lane... get it?!

But the setting really stands out. Dare I say it, the hallowed hall and shiny shores of Campus Drivers is far more glamorous than anything you’d see on Off Campus, Gossip Girl, and even Elite. The beaches, clubs, and architecture are beyond what we’re used to on collegiate shows like this. Here’s how they brought it to life.

Nope! The Prime Video series takes place at a fictional school on the French Riviera. However, according to Decider, Quill attended Carlone University (locally referred to as Campus Carlone) in Nice. So if you’re looking to study abroad, that might be a good place to start.

This is a major change from the book series, however, which takes place in the United States at a fictional school called Sycamore University. But since Quill herself is French, and went to school in France, this doesn’t feel like that big of a change.

The Prime Video series filmed on the French Riviera, a.k.a. the Côte d’Azur, in the South of France between Nice, Cannes, and Antibes. Lane’s apartment is filmed at the Marina Baie des Anges, a famous waterfront complex in Villeneuve-Loubet. And finally, Villa Eilenroc in Cap d’Antibes is where they filmed Belrose University.

While we can’t promise that your ride-share driver will become a fling (or more), these are fairly easily attainable vacation spots. Beaches like Les Ondes and Port Mallet on the Cap d’Antibes appear on the series and are open to the public. Start your planning now!

#6734. The Best Outfit Secret Is the One You Don’t See

Fashion editor and stylist Indya Brown on why the new Vanity Fair Lingerie’s Flawlessly Smooth Underwire Bra deserves a spot in your everyday rotation.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

“The most important part of your outfit is the one that no one sees, because that is where the look really begins,” says Indya Brown. As a fashion editor and stylist, she knows the little details make a big difference: the right fit, the right silhouette, and perhaps most important, a base layer that lets the rest of your outfit do its thing.

That’s the idea behind the latest Vanity Fair Lingerie Flawlessly Smooth Underwire Bra. It’s the kind of behind-the-scenes essential that makes getting dressed feel easier. Think of it as the quiet MVP in your top drawer: supportive, polished, and ready to work with whatever’s on the agenda.

It starts with lightly lined cups, which offer modesty and shape for a smooth look beneath clothing; rounded with a built-in flex panel under the bust, they’re designed to accommodate size fluctuations. Four hook-and-eye adjustment positions let you fine-tune the fit, while the straps are set closely together so they stay comfortably in place throughout the day.

Then there’s the Beauty Back four-way stretch back-smoothing technology, created to smooth lumps and bumps beneath clothes. The result is a foundation that feels supportive without stealing the spotlight. For Brown, that’s more than just a practical first step. “When you have a great foundation, it really changes how you show up,” she says. It’s the starting point for great style: helping a sharp blazer sit smoothly, a favorite dress falling just right, an everyday tee feeling a little more pulled together.

Choose the color that works best with your wardrobe, then let this everyday essential quietly do its job. Because when your bra is comfortable and your silhouette feels just right, you can focus on the outfit—and wherever the day takes you.
