#6733. Who Is Elizabeth Holmes’s Partner, Billy Evans?

This story contains spoilers for You Can See Everything.

When Nathan Fielder asked Elizabeth Holmes to document the days leading up to her serving her prison sentence for his new A24 documentary, You Can See Everything, the proposition meant that he would also be spending a large portion of the shoot with Holmes’s partner, Billy Evans.

The documentary spends time delving into Holmes’s public legal scandal with Theranos—the now-defunct biotech company that saw her committing investment fraud—but it also sheds light on Holmes and Evans’s relationship and how they have managed to thrive despite her sentencing.

As the documentary, co-directed by Lance Oppenheim, progresses, Fielder’s focus shifts from Holmes to Evans. “Weren’t you worried she was scamming you?” Fielder asked him at one point, to which Evans responded, “100 percent, but what does she have to gain from me?” When presented with the knowledge of his family’s wealth, Evans defended Holmes and said, “I think there’s a lot of different levels of wealth.” Things only snowball from there.

Evans’s grandparents, William and Anne Evans, own the Evans Hotel Group. Founded in 1953, Evans Hotels consists of three luxury properties, which include The Lodge Terry Pines, the Bahia Resort Hotel, and the Catamaran Resort Hotel and Spa. All three of the properties are located in California.

Evans’s LinkedIn profile revealed that he attended the Massachusetts Institute of Technology between 2011 and 2015. He earned his bachelor’s degree in economics.

Evans’s LinkedIn also revealed that he’s the CEO of Haemanthus, his self-founded AI-driven medical testing startup; he’s held the role since 2022. The company aims to achieve a mission similar to that of Holmes’s Theranos, which sought to provide blood diagnostics through one drop of blood with the Edison machine. Instead of using one drop of blood for diagnostics, Haemanthus is working to create a device that tests blood in addition to urine and saliva for the presence of diseases.

“This is not Theranos 2.0. Theranos attempted to miniaturize existing tests. Our approach is fundamentally different. We use light to read the complete molecular story in biological fluids, seeing patterns current tests can’t detect. Not an improvement. A different paradigm,” the company shared in a statement to Fortune in 2025, following reports of Holmes advising on the project. “Setting the record straight. Elizabeth Holmes has zero involvement in Haemanthus. We’ve learned from her company’s mistakes, but she has no role, now or future.”

In a 2023 joint interview with The New York Times, Evans revealed that he and Holmes fell for each other fast. “To say we immediately fell in love isn’t an overstatement,” he said.

Holmes, however, noted that Evans had some reservations about entering a relationship with her. “It had been two years of all this stuff written about me, and I think you get to know someone in a totally different way when you walk in with that skepticism versus if you meet when everything is sunshine and roses,” she said. “That allowed us to get to know each other in a really deep way.” The couple got engaged in August 2018.

Holmes gave birth to their son, William Holmes Evans, in 2021. By 2023, a court document revealed that Holmes gave birth to her and Evans’s second child, a daughter named Invicta.

Evans attended several of Holmes’s court dates, appearing by her side on the way to and from her hearings. In January 2022, she was found guilty of three counts of wire fraud and one count of conspiracy to commit wire fraud. She’s currently serving her sentence—which was reduced to nine years from 11 years—at Federal Prison Camp Bryan in Texas. She will be transferred to a halfway house in August 2027.

While Holmes was sentenced, Evans asked the court in November 2022 to shorten her sentence so she could enjoy more special moments with their son. “My heart is broken with the thought of spending any days away from Liz, for a future in which my son grows up with a relationship with his mother on the other side of glass armed by guards,” he said. Evans still visits Holmes regularly, with him telling People in February 2025 that it has been “hell and torture” at the prison facility. The tech CEO added that it “shatters” Holmes when she says goodbye to him and her family.

You Can See Everything makes some shocking revelations about Evans, like that he posts from Holmes’s X account while she’s in prison (for a while, it was without her knowledge), and that he arranged to be paid for his and Holmes’s appearance in the documentary, which he ultimately rescinded. (It’s unclear whether Holmes knew about the deal.)

During a screening of the film in L.A. this month, Fielder said of the couple, “Neither of them have seen the film. Elizabeth is still incarcerated; Billy hasn’t seen it.” He added, according to The Hollywood Reporter, that Evans “started trying to exert a level of control over the process that made it very hard to engage with him, and it was uncomfortable for us and also A24 and everyone. And I think we sort of had to stop—I think communication broke down basically, and we’ve been in that like state for a little bit. So it’s been challenging in that way.”

Evans confirmed that he hasn’t seen the film during an interview with the All In podcast, which was released on October 15. “I haven’t seen it. I might think this is the dumbest thing I have ever done,” Evans said, per Variety. He has seen the trailer and said that Holmes “looks insane” in it. Evans added, “Am I nervous? I have been through so many intense, surreal experiences that it takes a bit to raise my blood pressure. With this, there’s certainly concern, but it’s ultimately our decision.”

On November 3, Evans revealed the “one misconception” about his relationship with Holmes following the release of the movie: “That I somehow control Liz. Anyone who actually knows her would find that idea hilarious,” he responded to an X user.

Evans added, “She’s fiercely independent, strong-willed, and my best friend. We are partners in every sense. We challenge each other, disagree, and face the world together. Her closest friends are also some of my closest friends. If I ever mistreated her, they’d have my head.”

In a separate tweet, Evans revealed that he misses “everything about [Holmes].” He wrote, “I miss her presence. Her touch. The way she runs her fingers through my hair. Her patience. She makes me a better person. And without her, I feel an emptiness that’s hard to describe. People often say they need someone when what they really mean is that they want them. I’ve thought a lot about that distinction. I need Liz. She should qualify as a medically necessary prescription.”

On All In, Evans elaborated on why he wanted to make the film ahead of Holmes’s 11-year sentence. “She’s been prevented from speaking publicly in any way, shape, or form, and this would come out after that period in which she is now allowed to talk,” he said. “There’s been a lot of media produced on her. There’s been a lot of people who have told her story. That narrative is pretty ossified. The whole idea was: Here she is, see her and make up your own mind. It’s not to re-litigate the case. Practically, at this time she was not allowed to talk about the case, so she was gonna follow the rules and not talk about the case.”

He explained that his own loved ones have thought he was “crazy” for knowing—and now being in a relationship with—Holmes. “And they would meet her and spend time with her and see her in a totally natural environment, like going on a hike or helping a friend. Every opinion of her would radically shift,” Evans said. “That was the intention of the film. To help scale that up and allow other people to sit with her and make up their own minds.”

On November 15, Evans spoke with CBS News chief correspondent Matt Gutman for his first television interview about You Can See Everything, which aired on CBS Sunday Morning. During their conversation, he addressed the documentary’s portrayal of him as controlling and manipulative, pushing back against the characterization. “I’m deeply passionate about my family and about the work that I do, but no, I am not some controlling monster who’s leveraging his wife for insert-whatever-anybody-is-saying. No, I’m not those things,” he said.

Still, Evans said he did not regret participating in the documentary, even as he acknowledged the discomfort of seeing himself portrayed as a villain. “I would have preferred for that not to be the case, but it’s entertainment,” he told Gutman. “I didn’t get to control the edit.” He added that his primary concern was how audiences would perceive Holmes. “I think that my goal is to have people see Elizabeth. And when I watch that movie, I see the person that I love in exactly who she is.”

When it comes to his personal agreement to participate in the project, he said, “I think sometimes I take too big of risks.” Then, when Gutman asked for an example, Evans replied: “Inviting Nathan Fielder into your home for 34 days and trusting somebody to have the right to portray you in any way, shape, or form that they so choose.”

He said choosing to do this interview with CBS was an attempt to get his voice back. “You gotta learn really painful lessons sometimes in life….With Elizabeth going through everything she did in the press, the lesson that was ingrained in us is, ‘You can’t lose your voice.’”

During the interview, Evans also addressed the documentary’s controversial shower scene, in which he appears fully nude. He said he knew cameras were filming him but did not consent to the footage being shown. A24, the film’s production company, maintained that everything filmed was done with the couple’s consent.

Gutman also asked Evans about speculation that Fielder had developed romantic feelings for Holmes during filming. Evans disagreed with that interpretation, saying, “I think Nathan cares a lot about Elizabeth. And I didn’t get the impression for a minute that it was romantic.” When Gutman pointed to the scene of Fielder and Holmes playing in the water, Evans noted that he was nearby: “I was five feet to the right of them, also in the water.”

Evans further discussed the threats he says his family has received following the documentary’s release, calling them a “daily” and “terrifying” occurrence.

#6729. The Men on 'The Gilded Age' Don't Deserve Your Praise

They may be gentlemen, but their chivalry is sorely lacking.

It feels like television shows (and real life, let’s face it) are currently overrun with men who constantly fall short when it comes to matters of the heart. In the latest season of Nobody Wants This, many fans noticed how Joanne was constantly making sacrifices for their relationship while Noah just… wasn’t. The latest season of Love Is Blind also found the guys seriously wanting, with many failing to put in a bare minimum of effort to make a love connection. And it saddens me to say that this trend of low-effort men seems to have made its way to the fourth season of The Gilded Age as well. That’s right, my friends—not even the 19th century is safe from this epidemic of male apathy.

We may only be a few episodes into the season, yet already viewers have witnessed several examples where the men in the show are found lacking when it comes to their romantic partnerships. First, there’s Larry, who appears to have zero appreciation for the tough position Marian is now in after they spent the night together. Although he does suggest they get married, Marian worries it’s only out of obligation, and he seems to not really grasp the severity of the situation she’s in, that social ruin could be waiting for her if anyone found out about their night together.

Many fans online have been quick to come to Larry’s defense, criticizing Marian for self-sabotaging or playing the part of a martyr. But I disagree. Her concerns are valid and Larry is being ambiguous about his feelings. Now would be the perfect time for a grand romantic gesture on Larry’s part to show Marian how serious he is about their relationship. Where’s the big, swoonworthy “You have bewitched me body and soul” moment? Where is the standing-outside-her-window-with-a-boombox (or 1800s equivalent) scene? I want to root for them, but Larry is making it extremely difficult with his underwhelming devotion.

Then there’s Hector, who was praised by Bertha for choosing to save Gladys’s life over their unborn child’s during an extremely difficult labor. While it is nice to know how much affection he has for his wife, it never should’ve been his choice to begin with, so applauding him for “saving” Gladys feels like a very low bar to set for these male characters. I understand this was a customary practice at the time and him making the decision was more the doctor’s doing than anything else, but now he’s also gatekeeping that she can’t have any more children. Watching a man determine when a woman should know something about her own body feels outrageous in any time period. I’m not saying that Hector is a bad guy, but I don’t think he deserves so much admiration for doing the bare minimum.

Meanwhile, George Russell continues to deal with (or rather, not deal with) his ongoing laudanum addiction, which is already growing tiresome with each passing episode. His relationship with Bertha has never been worse. She is putting in all the effort to repair their marriage while he remains cold and distant, lying to her at every turn. I understand this change in his behavior is due to his drug dependency, but should that make all of his missteps completely forgivable? Bertha assured him at the end of Sunday night’s episode, “I won’t disappoint you.” But it is he who is the disappointment right now.

Unfortunately, not even Dr. William Kirkland can get a free pass, despite his impeccable behavior leading up to his wedding day. Buying Peggy a house, standing up to his mother, and refusing to let her dictate the wedding — those are all major green flags. Yet next week’s promo indicated William might pursue a new job in Washington, which feels completely out of nowhere considering he just bought a house for the two of them. Also, this is something he should probably consult his wife about before making any public declarations. Is the honeymoon phase over already?

Bottom line: Chivalry may not be completely dead on this show, but its pulse is weak at best and if these gentlemen don’t do something about it soon we’re going to have even bigger problems than prematurely pouring milk into someone else’s tea.

#6726. Zendaya Reveals How Tom Holland Supported Her on The Odyssey Set During Their Joint Appearance

Zendaya and Tom Holland stepped out over the weekend in support of The Odyssey—and the actress shared a sweet story about their time making the film.

On Saturday, November 14, the married actors joined their castmates Matt Damon, John Leguizamo, Charlize Theron, Elliot Page, and Himesh Patel for a special screening at the Academy Museum’s David Geffen Theater in Los Angeles. The event also included a post-screening conversation with the stars.

During the Q&A, Zendaya opened up about an especially nerve-racking moment on set and revealed how Holland helped her through it. The actress, who plays Athena in Christopher Nolan’s adaptation of Homer’s epic, recalled filming a night scene surrounded by performers she admired.

“I felt overwhelmed, to say the least,” she said. “I was on a night shoot, and I remember I came in and I was like, This is like a pinch me moment.”

She explained that she felt anxious filming in IMAX alongside so many talented people, including background and stunt performers “who are performing their hearts out.” But her husband’s support made a big difference.

“It helped that Tom came to set that day and he was in the corner like, ‘You got this; you’re doing good,’” she recalled.

For the event, Zendaya wore a romantic cream off-the-shoulder gown featuring ruffle detailing and an asymmetrical hemline. She finished the look with matching stilettos.

Zendaya previously spoke about her and Holland’s shared experience filming The Odyssey at its New York premiere in July. Zendaya told Extra she was “very grateful” Holland was there for her first day, adding that “walking onto a Chris Nolan set for the first time is no small feat, so I was happy to have some support.”

“The most toxic relationship of my life is now the bachelor LOL,” her post reads.

As far as exes go, Sam is also rumored to have dated former Wake Forest soccer star Tyla Ochoa (having confirmed he was dating a Wake Forest soccer player during a 2024 interview), but she has yet to weigh in on the casting news.

So…what is the truth? Is this man boring, toxic, both, or neither? Guess we’ll have to find out when The Bachelor season 30 hits ABC next year.

#6721. Why Taylor Swift Hasn’t Been Seen at Travis Kelce’s Chiefs-Falcons Game in Atlanta

After last Sunday’s Kansas City Chiefs football game, Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce re-created an early relationship moment as they left Arrowhead Stadium, driving off in a vintage car and waving to fans. This week, the Chiefs are playing against the Falcons in Atlanta—but as of 4:08 P.M. ET, Swift hasn’t been spotted in the crowd. The singer is also preparing for a big performance on Wednesday, when she’s slated to return to the CMA Awards stage in Nashville for the first time in 13 years. She has one major nomination as well: Single of the Year for her Toy Story 5 song “I Knew It, I Knew You.”

In September, she discussed how multifaceted the song’s lyrics are during an interview on iHeartCountry House Party. “I love that the lyrics of this song are really being delved into by the fans,” she said. “There are several lyrics that have more than one meaning to them. When you get to write a song that is this brilliant, that has many different themes, you only have so long in a song to convey and surmise some of this entire film that people just watched. Sometimes you’re trying to have a double meaning in a line.”

She cited one example: “The first line of the song is ‘I knew you through the daze of the blades of the grass in summer.’ The double meaning of that is...the word daze is spelled ‘d-a-z-e’ in the lyrics, but you could also, if you wanted to, think of it as days, ‘d-a-y-s,’ you could perceive it as that. I thought of it as like a childhood daze of like you look back and you see this sort of blurry, beautiful memory that’s kind of blurry at the edges, and the blades of the grass in summer harkens back to childhood and playing and using your imagination but also there’s the added meaning of—they’re toys—they can only see up to the height of the blades of grass.”

#6719. Your Expert-Backed Guide to Preparing for Top Surgery

From handling logistics ahead of time to a shopping list, experts share some of the best ways to make recovery from this major operation a little bit more manageable.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Getting ready to go under the knife can be scary—even when it’s for a procedure you’ve wanted for as long as you can remember. If you’re preparing for top surgery (a gender-affirming chest surgery to remove or augment breast tissue) right now, chances are you’re probably trying to ease your pre-op anxiety by googling every question you can possibly think of. And that’s normal! But it can also lead you down some deep, dark Reddit threads that’ll do nothing but amplify your nerves.

If you’re wondering how to prepare for top surgery without all the panic-inducing anecdotes, you’ve come to the right place. I chatted with three experts and got their best advice on how to get ready for the procedure while preserving your peace of mind.

Before we get into the tips, keep in mind that the most important part of preparing for top surgery is choosing the right surgeon for you. In addition to finding an experienced and gender-affirming doctor, you’ll want to pick someone who can give you the results you’re looking for. “The techniques your surgeon will use can vary quite a lot depending on your breast size and shape, the texture and quality of your skin, and your goals for the procedure,” explains Eric Plemons, PhD, an associate professor of anthropology and cochair of the Transgender Studies Research Cluster at the University of Arizona.

To make sure you’ll be happy with your results, he suggests watching YouTube videos showcasing before-and-afters and looking at post-surgery images online to help you get an idea of what you want. Then be sure to find a surgeon who understands your goals—if they seem like they’re not listening to you, ditch them. Once you’ve made an appointment with a surgeon you trust, here’s everything else the experts recommend doing to prepare for top surgery.

This might seem like a no-brainer, but it’s worth mentioning. Before your surgery, your doctor’s office will normally provide you with instructions on what to do before and after the procedure. They typically include info on dietary guidelines, activity restrictions, wound care, and any medications you might need. Make sure that you read all of the information carefully, and keep it handy for the recovery process. “Following these instructions diligently significantly improves surgical outcomes and ensures a smoother recovery,” explains Scott Mosser, MD, FACS, a plastic surgeon who performs gender confirmation surgery and the founder of The Gender Confirmation Center of San Francisco.

Top surgery is a major surgery, and you’ll likely spend the first one to two weeks at home resting after the procedure. So it’s a good idea to prep your space accordingly. Make sure you’ve got a cozy couch or bed setup ready, and consider getting a mastectomy pillow to make lounging and sleeping more comfortable. The Trans Health Program at Fenway Health also recommends getting other items like a long phone charger, a grabber tool, and a back scratcher to make recovery go a bit smoother.

Also, keep in mind that you’re not supposed to lift your arms above your head in the days following top surgery. To make that easier to navigate, consider moving food and essentials to lower, easy-to-reach spots, suggests Plemons.

The last thing you want to do is spend your recovery period stressing about going back to your job. Be sure you request enough time off to allow yourself to truly rest and heal. Exactly how much time you’ll need to spend OOO varies from person to person, so it’s a good idea to chat it through with your surgeon at one of your pre-op appointments.

One of the main factors that determines how long you’ll need to take off work is how physically demanding your job is. You’ll have restricted movement in the weeks after your surgery, and you won’t be able to lift heavy objects, which could impact your responsibilities, depending on your line of work. “I generally ask patients to identify the most physically strenuous aspect of their job, and together we determine an appropriate leave period,” explains Dr. Mosser.

For your post-op wardrobe, you’ll want a stack of loose-fitting shirts that are easy to put on and take off without raising your arms. (Yup, t-shirts might be off the table for a bit.) Button-up and zip-up options will be your best friends during the recovery period. The loose-fitting aspect is also important because you may need space for surgical drain tubes under your shirt. Tops with interior pockets can also be helpful if you do end up with drains, says Dr. Mosser. And heads-up: Your shirts might get stained during the healing process, so Plemons recommends grabbing some options from the thrift store so that you’re not worried about ruining them.

In order to help your skin adjust post-surgery, you may want to get an emollient-infused moisturizer like Bio-Oil or Palmer’s Cocoa Butter Lotion. As long as it’s okay with your doctor, you can even start applying these one to two weeks before the procedure to start prepping your skin, if you’d like. After the surgery, Dr. Mosser recommends using one of these moisturizers on your chest every morning and evening—but be careful to avoid your incision sites. This will help with skin relaxation and reduce the appearance of stretch marks, if that’s something you’re concerned about, he explains.

Double-check with your surgeon, but after any scabs from the surgery have fallen off, you should also be able to start using silicone scar tape. Dr. Mosser suggests using it for three months to help optimize healing.

If you vape or smoke cigarettes or weed, you’ll need to quit ahead of your top surgery. The American College of Surgeons recommends quitting smoking four to six weeks before any major surgery and staying smoke-free for at least four weeks post-op to lower your risk of complications, and most doctors require a similar timeline for top surgery.

The stakes are high: According to the Gender Confirmation Center of San Francisco, smoking increases the risk of anesthesia complications and postoperative complications like losing a nipple graft, which can require additional surgery to fix. It also delays healing, which can prolong the recovery process and make it more uncomfortable. Dr. Mosser requires all of his patients to stop smoking for at least three weeks before and after top surgery—no exceptions.

You’ll need some help taking care of yourself and your basic needs in the days following top surgery, so talk to your friends and/or family in advance to put together a plan. “We’ve consistently seen better outcomes in patients who have a positive, supportive presence in their lives. The best way to ensure this support is to communicate openly with loved ones in advance, set clear expectations about what assistance might be needed, and cultivate an environment of encouragement and care,” Dr. Mosser says.

Decide who’s going to drive you home after the procedure (you won’t be allowed to drive yourself), and think about who might be a good fit to help you with activities like getting dressed and showering the first few days, suggests Plemons. You’ll probably also need someone to help you with tasks like meal prep, medication reminders, carrying groceries, and caring for any pets for a bit.

In addition to logistical support, you’ll likely need some emotional support during the top surgery process. The procedure is often something you’ve been anticipating for a long time, and it can be an emotional experience. “Such a big event comes with big feelings about your body, about yourself, and about your future,” says Plemons. So think about who you plan to lean on for support as you navigate your feelings both pre- and post-op. Staying connected to your community—whether that be friends, chosen family, or an online community—is really important. “Even small moments of support, like check-ins or affirming conversations, can make a huge difference in feeling cared for during recovery,” adds Jesse Kahn, LCSW-R, CST, director and sex therapist at the Gender & Sexuality Therapy Center in New York.

“Top surgery is a significant emotional and physical milestone, and a range of feelings—excitement, anxiety, or unexpected emotions—are completely normal,” explains Kahn. They recommend giving yourself permission to feel and process your emotions without judgment or criticism. Your emotional support network can help with this, but if you’re already seeing a therapist you may want to schedule a session early on in your recovery. “[A therapy session] can be a good opportunity to just check in, look inward, reflect on what you’re experiencing, and give space for your emotions,” says Kahn. Practicing self-care, managing expectations, and doing activities that bring you comfort are also very important during the top surgery process, they add.

Give yourself something you look forward to by thinking about what you want to wear once you’ve healed. Buy a shirt in a style you’ve always wanted to wear, or plan out an outfit that’ll showcase your post-op body. “Choose something that will fit you well and show off the body you’re excited to have," suggests Plemons. "After soreness and swelling, it is amazing how gratifying it is to slip into that special shirt (or strut around in no shirt at all).”

This article is not intended to be a substitute for medical advice. Always seek the advice of your doctor or other qualified health professional with any questions you may have regarding surgery.

#6718. I Walked After Every Meal for Two Months, and My Digestion and Energy Levels Have Never Been Better

Experts weigh in on why sitting is the worst thing you can do after eating

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

It’s almost 9:00 p.m., and my work dinner in Manhattan’s SoHo neighborhood is finally coming to a close. As the people around me start to get up and call their Ubers, I head out the door to go home—on foot. After a lavish dinner, most people’s last thought is to get a brisk walk in, but for me, it’s crucial—not to hit a step count or burn calories, but as my version of a digestif, if you will.

My idea to walk after dinner came from a video I saw months ago from a TikTok nutritionist who touted a brisk 10-minute walk after meals as the key to boosting metabolism, regulating blood sugar levels, and helping your stomach digest food. My two favorite pastimes are eating and walking. I never thought the two would go hand in hand.

“Sitting briefly after eating isn’t inherently harmful, but prolonged, uninterrupted sitting can lead to a higher post-meal blood-sugar response compared with breaking it up with short walks,” says Elizabeth Sharp Edens, an internal medicine and obesity medicine physician, functional medicine practitioner, and founder of HMW Method. A 2022 meta-analysis in Sports Medicine found that light-intensity walking reduced post-meal glucose by almost 17 percent. The same study found that standing breaks after meals reduced glucose spikes by 9.5 percent on average. The TL;DR: Research says that any type of movement can improve glucose levels.

We know walking can do wonders—it can clear the mind, help you lean out, and even boost dopamine—but why is it a solution for feeling less full and avoiding afternoon crashes? “When you walk, your muscles use glucose for energy,” Edens explains. “Muscle contractions move glucose transporters called GLUT4 to the surface of muscle cells, helping draw glucose out of the bloodstream through a pathway that doesn’t depend on insulin.” Since your legs, the vehicle for walking, contain some of the biggest muscles in your body, it makes sense that walking effectively stimulates muscle contractions that move glucose transporters. “Walking breaks are a simple way to support blood sugar management while getting your steps in,” she adds.

While many rely on peptides, green powders, and “magic” supplements, walking is an easy, mindless (and free) way to improve digestion and prevent blood-sugar crashes. “Even two to five minutes after a meal can help, ideally before the glucose peak, typically 60 to 90 minutes after eating,” Kiran Krishnan, a research microbiologist, the founder and chief science officer of the Biome Learning Center, and Dose key opinion leader, adds.

I often joke after large meals that I will have to be rolled home. The good news: Walking can also reduce the overwhelming feeling of fullness. “Walking also helps the gut physically,” Krishnan says, citing a study that found that a slow walk after a meal significantly shortened the time it took the stomach to empty half its contents. “For people prone to bloating, mild [walking] cut intestinal gas retention from 45 percent to 24 percent and eased symptoms,” he adds. Plus, Krishnan says studies show reduced LDL cholesterol levels and improved liver function in those who walked after meals.

Then there’s good old gravity, says Caitlin Hosmer, a Maine-based registered dietitian and co-founder of MenoPantry. “Gravity helps too, which is part of why walking may help people who feel uncomfortably full or get reflux after meals,” she explains. “Gentle movement and staying upright help food move out of the stomach and along the digestive tract.”

Ten minutes isn’t long, but studies show it’s just as effective as a longer walk. A 2025 study published by PubMed Central found that a 10-minute walk lowered glucose measurements by 6 percent and a 30-minute walk lowered glucose levels by 5 percent, meaning just 10 minutes of walking is all you need to help reduce a post-meal glucose spike. If you can walk for 30 minutes, an hour, or even longer—that’s great. But just 10 minutes can work just as well.

What matters most is how quickly you get moving. “Glucose typically peaks 60 to 90 minutes after eating, and moving before that peak is what blunts the spike,” Hosmer says. “A walk puts your muscles to work just as glucose from the meal is flooding into the bloodstream.” At first, I questioned whether I had to walk after every meal. “Put your effort where it counts most: after your largest, most carbohydrate-rich meal,” Hosmer advises, adding that for most, that’s after dinner, but walking after all three meals is ideal.

My post-meal walks varied—some days I would walk for 30 minutes or even an hour, depending on how much time I had and where I was going next. But as I started incorporating them into my work schedule, especially on busy office days, I often had only 10 minutes to spare. When in a pinch, I would get up and walk around the office floor and, at home, do air squats, which engage the same large leg muscles as walking and can be just as effective as a post-meal walk.

It may feel strange to pencil in time to walk after every meal, but the benefits I have noticed surpass any inconvenience or awkwardness. I have fewer crashes after I eat, whereas I previously would feel lethargic after every meal. I’ve also noticed improved digestion. While this can be chalked up to a number of things (my beloved vibration plate, greens powders, or digestive enzymes), I always feel lighter and less full after a post-meal walk.

It’s also not meant to feel daunting or like a chore. “Your post-dinner walk isn’t a workout. It’s a way of helping your body handle the meal,” Hosmer says. “The one real ‘wrong way’ is skipping it because you think it has to be long or perfect. I often say something is better than nothing, and that’s especially true here. Even a few minutes of walking, started soon after you finish eating, can blunt the blood sugar rise, and it may help your digestion too.”

#6716. Sean Duffy Is a Perfect Reality-TV Politician. Too Bad for America.

As a fellow MTV veteran, I recognize that the transportation secretary has been practicing for this moment since his Real World days.

Yesterday, in the wake of Friday’s grounding of all flights in the continental United States and the weekend of cancellations and chaos that ensued, Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy made the rounds of the Sunday-morning political talk shows to make this face.

It’s been a tough few days for Duffy, and Duffy has been huffy. From Friday’s press conference with Markwayne Mullin, to his own microphonically challenged solo outing on Saturday, through all five morning-show appearances, Duffy has checked all the boxes the modern Republican official must: look aggrieved, place blame elsewhere, praise the president, be white. It’s all television, after all, and there’s a role to be played.

Sean Duffy has been at this for decades. You know he was on the sixth season of The Real World, and you know he met his wife and fellow Real Worlder Rachel Campos when they participated in an early season of what would become The Real World/Road Rules Challenge. But when you really go back and look, you’ll see that they’ve both been practicing for this moment since the ’90s. Game recognizes game; as a fellow alumnus of 20th-century MTV, let me walk you through it.

The Real World was born when MTV decided to try its hand at creating a weekly soap opera, in the style of the era-defining Beverly Hills 90210 and Melrose Place. But cable budgets being what they are, a full scripted show was untenable, so producers had the bold idea to do away with actors and writers entirely. The first couple seasons of The Real World kept the focus on art by casting young artists: Julie the emerging dancer amid the model and writer and singer-songwriters in season 1, Jon the wannabe Garth Brooks in season 2. But that went out the window quick, as producers and aspiring castmates figured out the same thing at the same time: The job was to stir the pot. So by season 3, deeply unpleasant bike messenger Puck was dropped into the San Francisco house to clash with saintly AIDS educator Pedro, with whom conservative Rachel initially refused to share a bathroom for fear of sink-based HIV transmission. In 1994.

By season 6, casts seemed chosen specifically to play a predetermined part and drive each other up the wall. Real World: Boston features Stanford med student Kameelah and ladies man Syrus, Catholic Texan Elka and Manhattan-born mess on wheels Montana. And then there was Sean Duffy, Wisconsin boy, lumberjack-games participant, and future secretary of transportation. Sean occupied the Cute Boy spot in the cast that had been held by Aaron in Los Angeles and St. Louis race car driver Mike in London. But this being 1997, he was also a different cultural archetype: the knee-jerk Rush Limbaugh–era conservative. Accent on jerk. Here’s a moment when Sean makes fun of Elka’s underwear and then immediately denies it.

Just straight-up, reflexively, smoothly denying he did something we just saw him do, because he did it on camera and into a microphone. Lying with a straight face. This would become a job skill.

It’s a little like something we saw Sean’s future wife Rachel do on her season. In an early episode, she engages in the hot, safe rebellious act of 1994 by getting her navel pierced. In a later episode, she gets a telephone call from her angry mother, who has seen the belly-button bauble in a photograph (a physical photograph, sent through the U.S. Mail—again, we lived differently in the ’90s). Rachel assures her mother it’s not a piercing but instead a sticker she put there. Forget the fact that, again, all of this is on camera and the mom is going to learn the truth soon enough. Watching back then, I remember thinking two things: One, this person lies very easily, and two, I think she might be one of those people who’s conservative just so they don’t upset their parents.

Anyway. Sean and Rachel did The Challenge and got married and had three hundred and fifty children. Sean became a congressman for Wisconsin; Rachel tried out for The View but never quite nailed it and now she’s on Fox & Friends sometimes. And because it all comes back to reality television and personal branding, the Duffy family spent a few weeks of Sean’s tenure as secretary of transportation to film a show. The Great American Road Trip was supposed to have aired on Fox Nation, but instead it got dumped onto YouTube, where most episodes are hovering around 10k views.

Also, Duffy’s son-in-law Michael Alfonso, who’s 26, is set to become the youngest person in Congress, having won a seat in Wisconsin’s 7th District in this month’s elections. Alfonso’s campaign was buoyed by generous campaign donations from numerous transportation companies, executives, and lobbyists, including a $2,500 contribution from Air Space Intelligence’s PAC in March. In June, the FAA awarded the AI start-up a contract worth up to $875.95 million. The company’s SMART system went nationwide days before the November 13 ground stop, though the FAA and the company said the outage was unrelated to SMART.

I’m sure it’s nothing. But I’m even more sure Duffy will say it is.

The way that John F. Kennedy understood television in the way Richard Nixon did not, Sean Duffy understands reality television in a way few others do. He’s lived it. He understands it as a marketing exercise. Knowing your brand, hitting the beats of the scene you’re supposed to play, showing up with a yearbook picture-day haircut and lying with a smile will get you far in this world. Whether he survives this turbulence or not, he’s learned that lesson.

Sean and Rachel have been at this for decades, but as viewers so have we. We can see them as they are: dishonest children, playing a role, begging for approval, just trying not to get yelled at.

#6712. HBO’s Harry Potter TV Series: What We Know So Far

Fifteen years after Harry Potter and the Deathly Hallows: Part 2, a reboot is on its way.

In the immortal words of Cornelius Fudge, “He’s back.”

Fifteen years after young wizard Harry Potter last appeared in the film Harry Potter and the Deathly Hallows: Part 2—and no matter how much vitriol has followed—Warner Bros. Discovery has summoned another incarnation of the Wizarding World saga. But there’s a twist! This time, the story will take the form of a seven-season television series (rather than an eight-film franchise) and will span a full decade. And there’s more! A new cast will fill the old roles made famous by the likes of Daniel Radcliffe, Rupert Grint, Emma Watson, and co.

Here’s a quick breakdown of what we know so far.

If you’ve watched the original film series, then you already know. Zaslav has promised that the reboot will be a faithful adaptation of the seven books in author J.K. Rowling’s series—meaning it will tell the exact same story as the movies, though with greater detail. Each season of the HBO series will cover the material in one of the books, so we can expect seven seasons in total.

Here’s how HBO Max is describing the series: “The stories from each of Rowling’s Harry Potter books will become a decade-long series produced with the same epic craft, love and care this global franchise is known for. The series will feature a new cast to lead a new generation of fandom, full of the fantastic detail, much loved characters and dramatic locations that Harry Potter fans have loved for over 25 years. Each season will be authentic to the original books and bring Harry Potter and these incredible adventures to new audiences around the world, while the original, classic and beloved films will remain at the core of the franchise and available to watch globally.”

The cast is “new,” meaning—thankfully—Radcliffe and friends won’t be expected to undergo any CGI de-aging. Instead, the roles they made famous will go to newcomers Dominic McLaughlin (as Harry Potter), Arabella Stanton (as Hermione Granger), and Alastair Stout (as Ron Weasley).

Of the hunt for these actors, showrunner/executive producer Francesca Gardiner and director/executive producer Mark Mylod released the following statement in May 2025: “After an extraordinary search led by casting directors Lucy Bevan and Emily Brockmann, we are delighted to announce we have found our Harry, Hermione, and Ron. The talent of these three unique actors is wonderful to behold, and we cannot wait for the world to witness their magic together onscreen. We would like to thank all the tens of thousands of children who auditioned. It’s been a real pleasure to discover the plethora of young talent out there.”

The announcement of these new lead actors followed multiple other rounds of casting reveals, starting in April 2025. The company first shared the identities of four adult actors, three of whom had already been reported by Variety and Deadline. They were as follows:

HBO also announced the cast for two smaller but nevertheless essential roles:

On June 9, 2025, nine more cast members were added to the ensemble:

On July 14, 2025, another round of cast members was announced:

On August 19, 2025, the new members of the Weasley family were announced:

On October 30, Deadline announced the addition of five more members to the season 1 cast:

Gardiner and Mylod, who have each worked on Succession, were enlisted for the new series, Variety first reported in June 2024. They are both serving as executive producers, along with Rowling, Fantastic Beasts and Where to Find Them’s Neil Blair, Brontë Film and TV’s Ruth Kenley-Letts, and David Heyman of Heyday Films. Gardiner will also be the writer and showrunner, while Mylod directed some episodes. In addition, Killing Eve writer Laura Neal joined the writers’ room.

The Harry Potter series is confirmed to release its first season—titled, appropriately, Harry Potter and the Philosopher’s Stone—on Dec. 25, 2026, on HBO and HBO Max.

Yes. On March 25, 2026, HBO Max released the first official teaser for the new series. Watch it below.

A second teaser trailer arrived on September 2, giving fans a better look inside life at Hogwarts. The clip opens with the Sorting Hat Ceremony in the Great Hall before the start-of-term banquet. Harry begs the Sorting Hat to not put him in Slytherin, leading to his Gryffindor placement (and a warm welcome from the rest of his housemates).

“Mr. Potter, I expect you to work hard. Find what sparks your interests, and make solid friendships,” Professor McGonagall tells Harry during a meeting in her office, which is spliced between scenes of him receiving his class schedule and finding the humor in Herbology class. But it isn’t an easy transition for The Boy Who Lived—he struggles in Charms classes, leading to his first set of insecurities. “Everyone thinks I’m this great wizard, but I really don’t know anything,” he says.

Harry later hits his stride as a Quidditch star, giving fans of the franchise a look inside the game as the famous boy wizard chases the golden snitch in the hopes of winning the match. Fans will also recognize The Burrow, Wizards Chess game, and the scary moment when the trio encounters Fluffy.

On Nov. 14, 2026, HBO released the first full scene from its upcoming Harry Potter series, offering a new look at the Hogwarts start-of-term banquet. The 90-second clip follows Harry, Ron, and Hermione as they attend their first feast in the Great Hall after the Sorting Ceremony.

The scene opens with students chanting for headmaster Albus Dumbledore, before Harry asks Ron whether Dumbledore is “a bit mad.” Ron replies, “Yep, totally off his rocker.” Dumbledore then magically conjures the banquet with a snap of his fingers.

The footage also includes a conversation between Dumbledore and Professor Minerva McGonagall about Harry’s placement in Gryffindor. McGonagall remarks on how much Harry resembles his father, James Potter, prompting Dumbledore to remind her, “Looks can be deceiving, Minerva. His nature is entirely his own.”

Although fans would love to see the original members make a cameo in the remake, Radcliffe told ComicBook.com in July 2023 that he won’t be pursuing an opportunity to be in the show. “My understanding is that they’re trying to very much start fresh, and I’m sure whoever is making them will want to make their own mark on it and probably not want to have to figure out how to get old Harry to cameo in this somewhere,” he said. “So I’m definitely not seeking it out in any way. But I do wish them, obviously, all the luck in the world, and I’m very excited to have that torch passed. But I don’t think it needs me to physically pass it.”

Radcliffe also told ScreenRant in February 2026 that he wants the young actors in the new series to be given a chance to succeed on their own terms.

“When these kids got cast, there was a whole thing around the internet [with] people saying we have to ‘look after these kids,’” he told the outlet, when asked what advice he’d give the new actors. “If everyone really means that one of the things you can do for me is not ask about us—me, Emma [Watson], Rupert [Grint]—all the time,” he added. “I would like not to be a weird spectral phantom in these children’s lives and just to let them get on because it’s going to be a new thing and it’s going to be a different thing.”

Without digging too deep into the Discourse™️, we can summarize two main reasons why some audiences aren’t convinced a Harry Potter reboot is, shall we say, necessary:

One is that Rowling will be involved not only as the source material’s creator but as an executive producer, meaning she’ll have some creative control over the reboot and will, of course, benefit financially from its success. That’s not good news for many fans who consider her controversial history of trans-exclusionary feminism to be a rot in an otherwise beloved franchise.

The second is that the story is far from new, nor is it particularly old. Harry Potter is practically inescapable in modern life. Whether it’s his books or his films or his merchandise or his theme parks or his exhibits or his video games, the boy with the lightning bolt scar is astoundingly difficult to avoid. A certain air of exhaustion (not to mention anger) has permeated even the most devoted corners of the Wizarding World fan base. What more can really be squeezed out of not just the same franchise, but the same story? Still, as time has passed since the announcement, it would seem more and more fans have come around to the idea of a Harry Potter adaptation that is deeply and thoroughly faithful to the books. As one commenter on the teaser trailer put it, “This is what I have always wanted. The books fleshed out in [a] TV series. No movie can do justice to how much there is in the books.”

Addressing the conversation around Rowling herself in a presentation timed to the series’ initial announcement, Casey Bloys, chairman and CEO of HBO & Max Content, had only this to add: “J.K. is an executive producer, and her insights will be helpful. We are in the Harry Potter business. The TV show is new and exciting, but we’ve been in the Harry Potter business for 20 years; this isn’t a new decision. We’re comfortable being in the Potter business. J.K. is a very online conversation…it’s very nuanced and complicated and not something we’re going to get into. Our priority is what’s onscreen. The Harry Potter story is incredibly affirmative and positive about love and acceptance, and that’s our priority, what’s on the screen.”

#6710. Joe Budden Can't Stop Betting on Himself

This is an edition of the weekly newsletter Tap In, GQ senior associate editor Frazier Tharpe’s final word on the most heated online discourse about music, movies, and TV. Sign up here to get it free.

There’s Joe Budden, and then there’s everyone else. Despite the proliferation of rapper-led podcasts and talk shows in the last few years (itself a subset of the ever-ballooning celebrity podcast bubble), there’s no question who leads the pack—at least that’s how Budden sees it. It’s hard to argue with him: in February, Budden’s self-titled and pioneering podcast will turn 12,. Not too long ago, he posted a screenshot that “accidentally” revealed his yearly take-home was a number in the low eight figures.

Through it all, there've been dramatic cast changes, tense moments with rappers, hilarious must-see breakdowns of big moments in culture, and several dust-ups with Drake, a frequent subject of Budden and his co-host’s. Being part-New Jerseyian, I’m one of the vocal minority that believe it’s a travesty to reduce Budden’s rap career to “Pump It Up” (this is never far from my Recently Played to this day). Our paths actually crossed during his time at Complex, when I served as a sit-in for test episodes of Everyday Struggle while he searched for the co-host that would eventually become Akademiks—and even helped them launch the show with a series of digital short skits I wrote and produced that are probably lost to the internet by now.

#6709. What Is Outercourse? Everything to Know About Sex Without Penetration

Because penetration isn't the only way to have really good sex (or really good orgasms).

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Blame it on the sexy movies, erotic books, or yeah—fine—porn, but at some point, we collectively decided that penetration was the main event. Not sure why (because, hi, have you ever received oral sex?), but everything else has sort of turned into “something to do until we get there,” and the “there” in question is penetrative sex.

Which is a pretty unfortunate way to think about sex, considering how much good stuff happens without penetration ever entering the picture.

Enter: outercourse. Think making out, dry humping, mutual masturbation, and all the other ways you can get off (or just get really turned on) without penetration. And no, these activities aren't just consolation prizes for when intercourse isn't happening. They can be the entire experience, orgasms and all.

The term gained popularity during the safer-sex movement of the 1980s, explains Good Vibrations sexologist Carol Queen, PhD, author of Exhibitionism for the Shy. It was developed “to help people differentiate between intercourse and all the sexy things that can be enjoyed without penetration,” she says.

Whether you're avoiding penetration, looking to switch things up with a partner, or just realizing you've been skipping over some genuinely excellent options, outercourse deserves a little more attention. Ahead, everything to know about what counts as outercourse, why it's worth exploring, and how to make the most of it.

Outercourse is an umbrella term for sexual activities that don't involve penetration, although the exact definition can vary. “The details of what this means is subjective from person to person based on their boundaries,” says Tyomi Morgan, certified sexologist and pleasure coach. “Some people consider outercourse everything but penis in vagina. Others exclude penetration of any kind including fingers, sex toys, and anal sex.”

Basically, outercourse can include everything from making out and sensual massages to dry humping, external sex toy play, and certain BDSM activities, says Queen.

“Many people think of outercourse as pretty vanilla, but depending on what you like, it can include as many slaps or spanks as light-fingered strokes. It is truly a variable type of play and very much worth exploring.”

And just because penetration isn't involved doesn't mean orgasms are off the table. Outercourse can be just as pleasurable as intercourse (if not more so...), and taking penetration out of the equation might even help you discover new ways to get off.

The two terms are sometimes used interchangeably, but there's an important distinction. “I am not crazy about the term 'foreplay,' because it implies that you are doing activities that precede the real main event,” says Queen.

The thing is, plenty of people have great sex without ever having intercourse. As Queen puts it, “Penetration is an option, not the definition of sex.”

Calling something outercourse rather than foreplay acknowledges that it can be the entire sexual experience, not just a lead-up to penetration. There's no requirement for anything to come before or after it.

There are plenty of reasons to explore outercourse, whether you're looking to switch things up or simply aren't interested in penetration. Here are a few benefits Queen and Morgan highlight:

Beyond the physical benefits, outercourse can also be a way to explore new forms of intimacy and connection with a partner. “Outercourse can be just as satisfying as penetration (if not more so),” reminds Morgan.

Generally speaking, outercourse can carry a lower risk of pregnancy and certain sexually transmitted infections (STIs) than penetrative sex, but that doesn't mean it's completely risk-free. The level of risk depends on what you're doing, whether you're using protection, and the type of contact involved.

For example, massages and touching over clothing generally carry little to no STI risk, while kissing, oral sex, and direct genital contact can transmit certain infections. Some STIs, including herpes and HPV, can spread through skin-to-skin contact, even without penetration. And while outercourse can reduce pregnancy risk, pregnancy is still possible if semen gets on or near the vaginal opening.

Talking with your partner about STI testing, using condoms or dental dams when appropriate, and practicing good hygiene can help reduce potential risks.

And, of course, consent is just as important during outercourse as it is during penetrative sex. “It's no less important to make sure your partner wants to do the things you're planning to do when engaging in external touch,” says Queen.

The easiest way to explore outercourse? Start doing more of the things you might normally rush through on your way to penetration. Take your time making out, explore different kinds of touch, and don't feel like you need to move straight to genital stimulation. Talk about your fantasies, give each other sensual massages, or see if you can bring each other to orgasm without penetration, recommends Queen.

And if you're looking to build even more anticipation, consider edging. “Try edging,” suggests sexologist Shamyra Howard, author of Use Your Mouth. “During mutual masturbation, attempt to bring each other to orgasm but stop right before orgasm occurs. Repeat this at least three times for an intense, mind-blowing climax.”

Ultimately, there's no right or wrong way to explore outercourse, as long as everyone involved is comfortable and consenting. Whether it becomes a regular part of your sex life or something you turn to when you're not in the mood for penetration, the point is to enjoy the experience without treating intercourse as the inevitable next step.

#6707. Zendaya Channels Dune in a Glittering Sculptural Gown at the 2026 Governors Awards

Zendaya is back on the awards show circuit in a glittering vintage gown meant to kick off her Dune: Part Three press tour. The actress attended this evening’s Governors Awards in a sculptural look from Giles Deacon’s fall/winter 2006 collection. She accessorized with a vintage Rolex watch and jewelry from Chopard.

Her stylist, Law Roach, wrote on Instagram that it’s her first Dune press tour look. The dress is even more stunning in motion:

The star posed alone, though her husband, Tom Holland, was at the event, too.

It marks her first awards show appearance since the Emmys in September, where she wore a glitzy Prada gown.

Dune: Part Three is the last of Zendaya’s five projects to come out this year. She first promoted The Drama and Euphoria season 3, which had April release dates, then The Odyssey and Spider-Man: Brand New Day, which debuted in July. In her ELLE Summer Issue cover story, she acknowledged it’s been a jam-packed 2026 with all the press tours.

“It’s going to be a long year, but what’s great is that I’m really proud of everything. I just really love my job,” she said. “But I think that after this, I’m going to have to take a little bit of a break. [Though] as soon as I take a break, I start to go crazy. And I’m like, ‘I need to work.’”

She also recalled filming all the projects and juggling the roles. “I remember being on set for Euphoria; it was a night shoot at a ranch,” she said. “I was so tired, but I was also learning my Chakobsa lines for Dune. And then I started writing out my lines to memorize for that quick turnaround trip I was going to make to Iceland [for The Odyssey]. It’s not like I have a lot of lines in The Odyssey, but I was working with Christopher Nolan! The most embarrassing thing in life would be messing up my lines, which did happen once.”

The Odyssey director Christopher Nolan, meanwhile, had nothing but praise for Zendaya’s performance, telling ELLE, “We would be in the maddest, craziest situations, just all of us fighting the elements, tearing our hair out, all these things going on, and she would sort of parachute in from her other job with this sense of true grace and poise.”

#6706. I Have Serious Questions About the 2026 Grammy Nominations

Is the Recording Academy out of its mind?

The Grammys, more than any other awards-voting body of the big EGOT four, is easily the most chaotic. Sure, we can shake our fists in the air all day about how Stanley Kubrick somehow never won an Oscar, or ask ourselves why the Tony Awards ever allowed themselves to become a place where SpongeBob SquarePants: The Broadway Musical leads with 12 nominees. But recalling Grammy events like Macklemore beating Kendrick Lamar, Esperanza Spalding claiming Best New Artist over Justin Bieber and Drake, and Beck taking home Album of the Year instead of Beyoncé, it’s a wonder just what happens between when the votes are submitted and when the winners are revealed.

This year? The Recording Academy announced on Monday, November 16, that Kanye West is a Grammy nominee once again. And there’s a single category where Bruce Springsteen, Phoebe Bridgers, and Charli XCX are lumped together, so I guess they’re considered basically the same kind of artist to voters. Plus, new ideas like “Best Asian Pop Music Performance” are moving forward despite immediately losing support from the most prominent artist in that category, and Best New Artist nominees can apparently put out their fifth studio album and still be considered. What’s going on?

So, before the Grammys decide to ignore all of our desires and complaints and just shower Taylor Swift with more little golden phonographs on February 7, 2027, let’s try to make sense of the 2026 Grammy nominees below.

The first thought that entered my mind when I saw this year’s nominees for Album of the Year was, Are we confirming who will be available to perform before announcing the nominees now? Because even if so, I think Bruno Mars would perform either way.

It’s very cool to see how women dominated the music field this year, especially because they truly did. Olivia Rodrigo, Phoebe Bridgers, and Rosalía all earning Album of the Year nominations is a no-brainer. Olivia Dean, who won Best New Artist last year, and Ella Langley, who wrote “Choosin’ Texas,” make up the rest of the list. (Did we really love that entire nineteen-track Langley album?) Plus, of course, the obligatory Taylor Swift nominations.

But were Bruno Mars and Leon Thomas—who are tied with seven nominations each after the leading women, including an Album of the Year nod for Mars—the two men with the best offerings this year?

Bruno Mars had a very good song on The Romantic with “Why You Wanna Fight?” The Luther Vandross-esque R&B ballad was everything you’d look for in a Song of the Year nominee. But instead, it looks like Bruno is going all in on “Risk It All” and “I Just Might,” the latter of which is more of a “Happy” by Pharrell Williams-type song that somehow isn’t the lead single of the next Trolls movie.

Sorry Madonna! Apologies Robyn, Tinashe, Slayyter, and every other pop artist who soundtracked our year on the dance floor with some of the best albums of 2026. Breaking into the top four major categories still looks like a no-go.

Instead, Madonna, Robyn, and Tinashe were relegated to categories such as Best Dance Pop Recording and Best Dance/Electronic Album, even despite critical acclaim. If you want a Record of the Year nod, try writing a song for Toy Story 5.

Don Toliver is up for Best New Artist. The Houston rapper, who was signed to Travis Scott’s label in 2018, released his fifth studio album, Octane, this year. He had a song on the F9 soundtrack in 2021—which isn’t even the latest Fast & Furious movie—and his 2023 album, Love Sick, featured James Blake, Future, and Justin Bieber. But here we are, in 2026, watching him compete for Best New Artist.

Equally puzzling: Remi Wolf is nominated for Best New Artist. The singer’s exceptional debut record, Juno, came out in 2021. And her best record to date, Mud, was released recently on October 9, just outside of the Grammy submission window that closed in late August. But following the logic of how Olivia Dean won Best New Artist last year even though Dean’s album is competing this year, it’s clear that the whole Best New Artist category needs some rethinking.

2026 wasn’t a banner year for rap. I can admit that. Sure, we had very good records from A$AP Rocky, Denzel Curry, Vince Staples, JPEGMAFIA, Isaiah Rashad, Genesis Owusu, and Baby Keem. But none of the musicians I listed above went recognized this year, except for Keem’s single “Best Rap Performance” nomination.

Instead, the Recording Academy nominated Wale’s Everything Is a Lot—the Washington, D.C., rapper’s best and most mature album of the last decade, but far less influential than Rocky’s or Keem’s releases. And if not for Drake’s long-running boycott, the absence of Iceman’s “Janice STFU” just looks silly. But if you want to talk ridiculous, let’s see how Rap Album of the Year nominee Kanye West plays out for the Recording Academy over the next couple of months.

Harry Styles won Album of the Year for Harry’s House in 2023. He beat out Un Verano Sin Ti by Bad Bunny, Renaissance by Beyoncé, Mr. Morale & the Big Steppers by Kendrick Lamar, and 30 by Adele. It was a major upset. And just last month, the former One Direction member wrapped a historic thirty-night residency at Madison Square Garden in New York City in support of his latest album, Kiss All the Time. Disco, Occasionally.

Now, I’m not saying Kiss All the Time is a masterwork, but it’s strange that Styles didn’t receive a single nomination for his Harry’s House follow-up. “Aperture” was very catchy! Taylor Swift can’t sneeze without the Recording Academy inventing a new category for her to compete in. But an artist who just won Album of the Year can find himself completely shut out, even if his latest record is a success?

The global K-pop megastars reunited this year following mandatory military service in South Korea for one of the genre’s most-anticipated albums of all time, ARIRANG. The record paid homage to the popular Korean folk song of the same name while also pushing the K-pop genre forward with hip-hop-heavy material. ARIRANG’s fourteen tracks also filled the top fourteen spots of Spotify’s global Top 50 chart on its first day of release on March 20. I’ll stop there, but there are a hundred more ways to say that it was one of the biggest albums of the year.

But when the Recording Academy announced a new category this year, “Best Asian Pop Music Performance,” the group made the decision not to submit for the Grammys this year. The move was widely regarded as a protest of the new category, which many interpreted as a ghettoization to keep the rise of popular Asian acts away from the general field categories. “I hope that music can be heard and loved for what it is, rather than being divided by region or language,” the group posted on social media.

On Monday, November 16, the Academy went through with the category anyway, announcing nominees including Lisa from Blackpink, the Japanese pop-metal group Babymetal, the K-pop group IVE, and the Filipino girl group Bini, among others. But with BTS joining Drake in his boycott of the Grammys, I wonder how the Recording Academy will fare without two of the world’s most popular acts entertaining its awards show.

The Great Divide, a seventeen-track rock album that turned Kahan into one of the most popular touring musicians of 2026, was considered a favorite to receive a nomination in every major category this year. Songs like “American Cars” dealt with past failures and road trip blues, while lead single “The Great Divide” tackled how fame added extra struggles to many of his already-strained relationships. And Kahan had the resume to become a major category contender this year. He had a Number One record! He did the full press tour, he performed on Saturday Night Live, and he sold out stadiums all across the country.

But it must have been an omen when Kahan sang that “California’s so much more than some awards show” on the deluxe edition’s “Orbiter,” because he received just one nomination on Monday for Best Contemporary Folk Album. It’s a shame.

Best Album Cover is back. The category returned last year and accurately awarded Tyler, the Creator’s zany Chromakopia artwork, which featured the artist wearing a black, ceramic mask. He told Complex at the time that it symbolized how the new music was “so honest, that I think I had to wear a mask of my own face to get some of that sh*t out.”

But what about this year’s nominees? Well, Mavis Staples’s Sad and Beautiful World artwork depicts the artist sitting at a table. Interpol’s This Mirror Weighs a Ton artwork is a chandelier made of security cameras, and Lily Allen’s West End Girl is a painting of the artist wearing a polka-dot jacket. Are these nominees... the best album covers of the year?

Charli XCX’s photograph of Martin Scorsese, John Cale, and Marc Jacobs was easily the much-talked-about album cover of the year, even though Music, Fashion, Film wasn’t nominated. Olivia Rodrigo’s upside-down swinging for you seem pretty sad for a girl so in love is definitely one of the more memorable covers that come to mind. Same goes for Robyn’s blurry and nude Sexistential photo, the politics behind Kneecap’s Fenian, Thundercat biting his dark leather jacket on the cover of Distracted, or even a pregnant Latto posing with a baby tiger for the Big Mama cover ahead of her cheetah-themed baby shower.

We might not have a specific shade of green to color this year and make Best Album Cover an easy choice, but if you’re going to bring the category back? At least wow us.

#6704. 10 Different Types of Sex You Should Know About (and Definitely Explore)

Because there's a whole lot more to sex than penetration.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

For something people talk about, think about, and Google pretty much constantly, sex has a surprisingly narrow reputation. Say the word, and chances are your brain immediately jumps to penetration. Which, sure, can be great. But treating intercourse as the default definition of sex leaves out a whole lot of ways people experience pleasure.

Because here's the thing: Sex isn't just one specific act. It can involve mouths, hands, toys, technology, or absolutely zero penetration. And depending on who you ask, what counts as sex might look completely different.

“Sex is a mental, physical, spiritual, and emotional erotic connection shared between those involved,” explains sexologist Shamyra Howard, author of Use Your Mouth. “It's not just about genitals and mouths.”

For one person, sex might include any kind of sensual touch, while someone else might define it as an activity involving penetration. Neither is inherently more valid. As psychotherapist Kristie Overstreet, PhD, puts it, “there isn't one that is ‘better’ or a ‘more real’ type of sex than the other.”

Ultimately, what matters is that everyone involved is on the same page about boundaries, expectations, and consent. Whether you're curious about trying something new, expanding your definition of sex, or just figuring out what all the different sex terms actually mean, here's a breakdown of 10 types of sex worth knowing about.

Masturbation, aka the act of pleasuring yourself, is hands-down one of the best types of sex you can have. It can involve stimulating your own genitals, anus, nipples, or other erogenous zones. Masturbation can also be called jerking off, touching yourself, playing with yourself, or self-pleasure.

As for the benefits? Masturbation can help relieve stress, build confidence, and figure out what types of sensations make you orgasm, says Step Tranovich, founder of sex toy company Cute Little Fuckers.

While masturbation is typically done solo, you can also incorporate a partner through mutual masturbation, where you masturbate in front of each other. According to OkCupid spokesperson Crysten Curry, mutual masturbation can help you learn more about your partner's likes and dislikes while offering a relatively low-risk way to explore pleasure together.

Manual sex, also called digital sex, involves using your hands to give your partner sexual pleasure, explains sex therapist Tara Abrol. This can include fingering, hand jobs, or stimulating the genitals, anus, and other erogenous zones.

While manual sex might be a part of your usual foreplay routine, it shouldn't be ignored because it has major headliner potential. “This is a great option for your sexual buffet so you connect in a different way and find other ways to pleasure one another,” says Overstreet. “It can be very exciting to watch one another and it can build trust.”

Foot sex can involve using your feet to stimulate your partner’s genitals (often called a foot job) or stimulating your partner’s feet to the point of orgasm. This could mean anything from touching and rubbing to incorporating toes into penetrative play.

For people who are into foot worship, feet can be a major source of arousal. “Just as other parts of the body are turn-ons, for some, feet are the turn-on,” says Overstreet.

When people say “intercourse,” they typically mean vaginal intercourse, which is what happens when a vagina is penetrated by a penis. But vaginal penetration can also involve a strap-on or dildo.

Just so we're clear, though, if this is the only way you view sex, it might be time to expand your definition. Mainly because many people with vaginas don't orgasm from penetration alone, so incorporating other types of stimulation during intercourse can make a big difference.

Another way to up the pleasure component of intercourse is to try different positions, says Curry. Try cowgirl or the coital alignment technique, which are two positions that allow for extra clitoral stimulation. This can help increase the chances of orgasming during intercourse.

As the name suggests, outercourse is pretty much the opposite of intercourse in that it generally doesn't involve penetration, explains certified sexologist and pleasure coach Tyomi Morgan. Think kissing, cuddling, body rubbing, mutual masturbation, dry humping, boob sex (rubbing a penis between breasts), and discussing each other's fantasies.

Erogenous zone touching, such as stimulating the nipples to climax, also falls under the outercourse umbrella, notes Howard. And even though some people don't consider outercourse to be sex, Morgan says it can absolutely be just as satisfying, if not more so, as penetrative sex, especially for people who have a heightened sense of touch.

As you might have guessed, using sex toys to sexually stimulate your or your partner’s body is called toy sex, explains Tranovich. These toys can be anything from wand vibrators to dildos to butt plugs to rabbit vibrators, and they can be used alone or with a partner. Since it's the use of a toy that makes toy sex a thing, it can happen alongside other types of sex, like intercourse, outercourse, and masturbation.

Sex toys can also help you figure out what feels good and how to have more satisfying partnered sex, explains Curry, giving you the confidence and knowledge to explore different sexual experiences. Plus, toys can make reaching orgasm easier by providing different types of stimulation.

As the name suggests, oral sex involves using your mouth, lips, or tongue to stimulate a partner's genitals or anus, explains Tranovich. Oral sex is sometimes called going down on someone, giving head, giving a blow job (if the receiver has a penis), or eating out (if the receiver has a vulva).

“So many of our human sensations begin in our face: taste, smell, sight. So giving oral sex immerses our senses in the erotic experience, increasing arousal and making people more present in the experience,” says Tranovich.

While anal sex typically refers to anal penetration, which is when someone's anus is penetrated by a toy, fingers, or a penis, Tranovich says there are a lot of other types of anal play to explore as well, all of which can fall under the anal sex umbrella.

“There are tons of nerves around the outside the anus, so you can have anal sex without needing to put anything inside you,” explains Tranovich. So something like massaging around the anus with your finger or a toy can definitely count as anal sex if that's how you want to define it.

What makes anal sex so great is that everyone has a butt, so it's an option for people across the gender spectrum. If you're curious but nervous about trying it, you can start with external stimulation, like gently touching or massaging around the anus, before deciding whether you want to explore penetration.

Also, don't forget the lube. The anus doesn't self-lubricate like the vagina does, so using plenty of lube is especially important for comfortable anal penetration.

Virtual sex is all about using technology to get off with a partner who isn’t physically with you, explains Curry. Usually accompanied by masturbation and/or sex toys, virtual sex can include everything from sexting to phone sex to FaceTime sex and even attending virtual sex parties.

Virtual sex can be especially appealing for people in long-distance relationships or partners who are temporarily apart, whether for work, travel, or other reasons.

If this is a type of sex you're interested in exploring, consider incorporating an app-controlled toy so your partner can control the settings from afar, adding another layer of intimacy to the experience.

BDSM is kind of a catch-all acronym for different aspects and types of power kinks, sex therapist Liz Powell, PhD, previously told Cosmopolitan. “The B and D are for bondage and discipline, the D and S are for domination and submission, and the S and M are for sadism and masochism,” Powell explained.

The key here is that BDSM involves consensual exploration of power, sensation, and control, depending on the activities involved. As for what BDSM sex actually is, it can range from wearing a blindfold to experimenting with restraints or spanking. Think kinky + consensual.

Just make sure you talk about boundaries, wants, needs, hard limits, and safe words before engaging in any BDSM activities.
